## Global Data Watch

0 Powell’s hike aversion, US labor market cooling boost global risk appetite

● Despite April PMI dip, global industry on a recovery track

• Western Europe to join EM in cautious easing

Next week: Services PMIs; Riksbank, COPOM ease 25bp; Banxico holds

## Opportunistic disinflation, redux?

Global growth and inflation far exceeded our expectations last quarter and shook both market and central bank confidence that a significant interest rate decline is in the offing. Global GDP is tracking a 3.3%ar gain in 1Q24, a full %-point above its potential pace and our forecast at the start of the year. Although US growth downshifted, it remained a powerful demand engine generating rapid import and job gains. With Western Europe rising from its slumber and strong increases posted across Asia, 1Q24 global GDP growth outside the US and China rose at a 2.6%ar, its fastest gain in eight quarters. Core CPI inflation, meanwhile, accelerated to a 3.5%ar last quarter (ex. China and Türkiye). In contrast to the growth surprises, this acceleration was concentrated in the US and Euro area, which posted annualized gains of 4.4% and 3.2%, respectively, last quarter.

These outcomes reinforce our view that central banks will have limited space for easing this year. But we see 1Q24 momentum fading and anticipate global GDP and core CPI gains will moderate to a 2.5%ar and 3%ar, respectively, this quarter. While this week<sup>’</sup>s initial April activity releases, the global manufacturing PMI and US jobs, point to slowing, it is too early to confidently gauge current-quarter growth momentum.

Along with this anticipated downshift, the outlook depends on how broader financial conditions respond to last quarter<sup>’</sup>s surprises and interest rate repricing. Financial markets have been impressively resilient so far (Figure 1). To a large degree, this resilience is being encouraged by central bank guidance. The ECB is firming a commitment to a June easing despite a 1Q24 upside inflation surprise.

This week<sup>’</sup>s FOMC communications were also supportive. While acknowledging that the recent disappointing progress in bringing inflation down will likely delay the start of easing beyond midyear, Chair Powell expressed patient optimism. He maintained his view that the Fed<sup>’</sup>s policy stance is restrictive and that labor markets are moving toward better balance. He envisioned three likely paths ahead: a path where inflation remains persistent, and they “hold off on rate cuts;<sup>”</sup> a path where they regain confidence that inflation is coming down and they cut, and a path of unexpected weakening in the labor market that prompts cuts.

Powell<sup>’</sup>s resistance to discuss rate hikes hints at the opportunistic disinflation strategy applied in the 1980s. Under this approach, the Fed does not act in the face of moderately elevated inflation. Instead, it waits for external circumstances (favorable supply shocks and unforeseen recessions) to return inflation to target while standing by to aggressively resist inflation turning too hot. Market reaction to this message was understandably favorable. But Powell is unlikely to be speaking for a committee that may not be as patient.

Maintaining a balance between growth, inflation, and financial conditions will be challenging and we do not have conviction in the medium-term sustainability of the expansion. This week<sup>’</sup>s news showed a 1Q24 rebound in US unit labor cost gains and a decline in Euro area GDP deflator growth last quarter, which is consistent with downward pressure on profit margins. This in turn could sour business sentiment and amplify any slowing in growth. At the same time, easing financial conditions could over-stimulate activity and raise inflation above the opportunistic comfort zone.

## Cautiously upbeat on global industry

Amidst noisy activity readings we see encouraging signs that global industry is gradually recovering as final demand remains resilient and inventory drags are fading. This enthusiasm is tempered only a bit by this week<sup>’</sup>s disappointing manufacturing PMI report for April. A 0.4pt dip in the output PMI comes after three straight strong gains, leaving the level consistent with still-okay 1.6%ar growth in global factory output—just a touch below our 1H24 forecast of 2%ar growth. The hard activity data are still tracking a notable improvement through the first three months of the year (Figure 2). After ending 4Q23 on a very soft note, global retail sales spending has been choppy but has accelerated on net. Business equipment spending also looks to have accelerated this year, continuing a trend up after stalling around mid-2023.

The firming in demand has outpaced production growth, and this has ended the inventory correction that weighed on manufacturing around the turn of the year. On balance, the mix of final demand and inventories looks encouraging for further

gains in output in 2Q24. However, the April PMIs came with a more cautionary note than just the small dip in the output component. A large 2.6pt tumble in the investment goods PMI for last month slashes our global capexnowcast by 3.4%-pts to a now-soft 1.6%ar for this quarter. It is still early in the quarter, but the momentum loss would be a concern if sustained.

## EM Asian exports leading global industry

In contrast to the modest dip in the April global reading, the April PMI data were on balance positive for Asia, with the April new export orders PMI rising back to 2021 levels. This bodes well for trade, with exports already accelerating materially in recent months (Figure 3). The region<sup>’</sup>s tech production took a step down in 1Q but this seems more a breather from the booming gains in 2H23 than a break in the trend. Indeed, underlying final demand has remained solid even as non-tech output remained soft. Within the region, Japan<sup>’</sup>s goods sector looks to be emerging from a long slumber following the shocks of earthquake-related disruptions and auto plant shutdowns. Chip production is at decade highs, reflecting tailwinds from a regional tech cycle upturn and new domestic production facilities coming online.

Figure 3: Asia new export orders PMI and exports
![](images/27f856e79f9d050509abe471baf9455a60b884e1dd6c3adb21a7f68f1f16f49b.jpg)

Much of Mainland China<sup>’</sup>s 30%ar surge in 1Q24 exports reflects a growing market share in non-G3 markets, notably other emerging markets. While some of the trade with LatAm (especially Mexico) and ASEAN likely reflects trans-shipment flows, the rise in trade with other regions including Brazil, Russia, and the Middle East likely reflects strong final demand for Chinese goods. Follow-through in the rest of the Asia region has been mixed, with the weakness in Korea<sup>’</sup>s March IP spread across both tech and non-tech production. At the same time, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s capex has rebounded after contracting in recent quarters, reflecting an improving export outlook.

## Shallow easing starts in Western Europe

Next week, the Riksbank should follow the SNB as the second DM central bank to enter easing mode. While other Western European central banks are on track to ease, the risks have shifted in the direction of a shallow adjustment through the end of this year.

Currency weakness is the primary constraint in Scandinavia. For Sweden, sluggish growth, a weak labor market, and a sharp fall in pricing indicators justify the 100bp easing we forecast this year. However, the slide in the SEK could reduce the size of this move. A weaker NOK and a high wage settlement prompted Norges Bank to shift forward guidance at this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, and we have pushed our forecast for a first cut from September to December.

The Euro area is on track to ease in June but we are lowering our sights for 2H24 as the Euro area delivered upside surprises on growth and core inflation. In light of improved growth prospects and upside inflation risks, we have slowed our expectations for ECB cuts to 75bp.

The BoE is on track to ease next quarter but will likely show little urgency to cut rates at next week<sup>’</sup>s meeting. Near-term services inflation and pay growth are thus tracking slightly above the Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast, and revisions to growth and the labor market are likely to reflect more resiliency. We thus think the aim is to leave the impression that a first cut is likely around August, with only some probability of a cut in June.

## Testing time for the BoJ

Pressures on the yen reached a boiling point after the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s meager defense last Friday. As USD/JPY breached the optically-important 160 threshold going into the long Golden Week holiday, it looks likely that Japan<sup>’</sup>s Ministry of Finance intervened several times. Such large actions are not sustainable without more fundamental support on rates, a fact that markets are now likely to keep testing. However, given sticky inflation and rising wage growth, we think that markets are underestimating the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s path through year-end. In our baseline, we continue to project two more hikes by December, bringing the policy rate to 50bp, and we look for the tightening cycle to extend gradually through 2025. Nevertheless, sustained currency pressure could telescope this trajectory.

## EM still on cautious easing path

EM central banks have turned more cautious but outside of Asia, where wider rate spreads to the US have pressured currencies, most remain on course for further easing. The USD is stronger since the start of the year but has not broken out of its recent range and global financial stress remains minimal. Indeed, EM portfolio inflows look to have rebounded after contracting last month (Figure 4).

In EM Asia, growth remains subpar and inflation is running within CB target ranges as evidenced by this week<sup>’</sup>s soft

April CPI reports in Indonesia and Korea. However, currency concerns have led us to push rate cuts back in Indonesia (from 3Q24), with risks of another near-term rate hike. With more ample FX buffers, the BoK appears less constrained, and we still see a cut in 4Q24. In CEE, central banks are still easing but some are losing conviction amid rebounding growth and sticky core inflation. The CNB this week cut another 50bp but with hawkish messaging, and we look for easing to slow to 25bp next month. Next week, we see Poland<sup>’</sup>s NBP on hold; our call for 50bp of cuts later this year crucially hinges on Fed and ECB easing. In Romania, domestic inflation pressures point to risks of delayed easing.

Figure 4: Daily EM non-resident portfolio flows
![](images/f5b2e75ceb490a800287355446d2713590c44b1aeb61ecb5e37904d4c7aecdfa.jpg)

In LatAm, high real rates argue for further cuts, but FX volatility is keeping central banks cautious. Colombia<sup>’</sup>s BanRep stuck to a 50bp easing pace this week and we see it continuing at a steady clip. Brazil<sup>’</sup>s COPOM is likely to slow its easing pace to 25bp next week amid tighter global conditions, a 1Q growth recovery, and a lack of fiscal restraint. In Mexico, fiscal policy is loosening and the peso becoming more sensitive to external conditions. We thus expect a strategic pause next week that continues through the August meeting. Peru<sup>’</sup>s BCRP should stay put next week and should resume easing in June with another 25bp cut.

## Policy supports China as housing drags

A tightening in policy after China<sup>’</sup>s strong 1Q GDP bounce (to 7.4%ar) led us to mark down 2Q growth to a 4%ar, but weakness in March activity data kept risks skewed to the downside. After this week<sup>’</sup>s Politburo meeting, growth risks appear more balanced as policymakers look attentive to growth drags and show willingness to act if needed. On housing, the focus was on addressing the inventory overhang and boosting public rental housing investment. Despite the constructive steps, we maintain housing will be a drag for some time. We expect that, without a major change in housing policy, the housing market decline will continue in 2024, with key volume indicators falling another 10%, stabilizing only in 2025, with the macro drag persisting into 2026.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth moved sideways this week at 2.4%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster ticked up 0.1%-pt to 2.5%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

About 40% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in 75% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests a downshift in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 1.6%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The April global manufacturing PMI headlined this week<sup>’</sup>s data. While the global output index slipped 0.4-pt last month, it remains at a level consistent with solid 1.6%ar growth in global factory output. We see manufacturing continuing to recover in the months ahead, boosted by resilient consumer spending amidst rising confidence.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds rallied with the US outperforming amid the softerthan-expected labor market data. While the FOMC statement contained some hawkish changes in language, Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s Q&A took a less hawkish tone. Though the softer data and somewhat less hawkish tone by Powell is supportive for duration, there is still event risk given supply and approaching CPI data. Our strategists stay neutral duration outright and prefer 5s/30s steepeners to position for eventual easing as well as higher term premia.

In the Euro area, while the ECB has signaled a June cut, our economists see that domestic data, e.g. sticky services inflation and little sign of a pickup in productivity, pose a challenge to subsequent cuts. They expect three cuts in 2024, rather than four previously. Our strategists retain a mediumterm bullish view, but prefer proxies via curve steepeners in 10s/15s Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps. In the UK, we see no changes from MPC meeting except more dovish dissent, but forecasts changes should reduce urgency around rate cuts. Our strategists keep 1Y/1Yx1Y SONIA curve flatteners.

In EM, the global growth backdrop suggests a broadening beyond the US, but the inflation backdrop remains dominant in the near term. Our EM Client Survey suggests a modest reduction in EM local market duration to close to neutral levels. Our strategists stay neutral local bond duration overall. They hold selective OWs via Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Czechia and Poland, offset by a short duration overlay as well as an UW in Thailand (EM Fixed Income Focus, Apr 25<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

April 2024 issuance trend across US HG, US HY, Loans and CLOs. US HG gross issuance was up 11% versus the 4yr average (ex-2020) in April. YTD issuance makes it the second most active year after 2020 and brings us to 48% of our FY24 forecast of \$1.3tr. Fifty-one percent of gross supply last month came from Non-Financials while the rest comes from Financials. HY supply in April was only modestly below 1Q24<sup>’</sup>s torrid pace, despite the spike in yields. Leveraged loan gross issuance is off to its second most active start on record behind only 2017<sup>’</sup>s pace amid a surge in repricing and refinancing. YTD US CLO gross supply is now the second fastest pace ever (the record is in 2021 YTD), and new supply is the fastest ever (US High Grade Corporate Bond Issuance Review, May 2<sup>nd</sup>).

In our view, euro credit will remain range bound in coming weeks. Euro credit traded essentially unchanged over the past week, with euro IG and HY cash 1bp and 7bp wider, respectively, and CDS indices slightly tighter. In our view, credit markets are signalling that they can handle a <sup>‘</sup>higher-for-longer<sup>’</sup> environment, provided that the narrative does not shift toward a renewed hiking cycle. Consequently, we recommend selling volatility, and sell an iTraxx Main 55bp June straddle in our top trade portfolio (European Credit Weekly, May 3<sup>rd</sup>). The outlook for european HY defaults beyond 2024 is clouded by conflicting narratives. Topdown macro data point to a bounce in Euro area activity, consistent with default rates remaining contained in the low-single-digits. The bottom-up news flow is less encouraging, with the quantity of distressed debt remaining persistently high despite tight index spreads, and a growing number of high profile restructuring candidates. The base case is that a large portion of restructuring activity arising from the current distressed cohort will slip into 2025. We hold our FY24 default rate forecast at 2.5%, and set a FY25 target of 3% (High Yield Talking Points, May 2<sup>nd</sup>).

## Currencies

Taking a breather on fading US exceptionalism (for now). Our bullish USD view has been based on two motivating pillars: carry and US exceptionalism. The former remains intact, but the latter appears to be losing some sheen. Combined with crowded positioning and valuations make it prudent to tactically reduce USD length. But we caution it is still too early

for USD bears to declare victory given still-firm US inflation and lack of visibility on the durability of the recovery outside the US as well as upcoming US elections.

Macro Trade Recommendations: Continue to scale down USD exposure after NFPs, but keep net length. Take loss on USD/CAD. Sold CAD/NOK and added 3m EUR/NOK put spreads mid-week; take profit on NOK/SEK longs. Still long USD (vs EUR, CHF, CNH, GBP) but primarily in options. We are MW EM FX (FXMW, May 3<sup>rd</sup>).

## Commodities

The oil demand growth for April remains below our expectations. The underperformance in the realized demand vs. our expectations was due to weaker US gasoline consumption, where US retail gasoline prices were a 20% increase from January lows. Europe<sup>’</sup>s oil demand continues to oscillate in a 100 kbd range around our published projection. China oil demand is below our estimates for the month but demand improved over the week. Japan<sup>’</sup>s total oil demand contracted driven by lower naphtha and fuel oil demand. Global jet fuel demand expansion moderated for the week while global flight activity improved as travel demand increased ahead of Labor Day holiday in China and Europe (Oil Demand Tracker, May 1<sup>st</sup>). Our fundamental view on oil has not changed. Brent averaged \$89 in April vs our fair value of \$86, and May continues to screen at \$88, ahead of the OPEC meeting on June 1. The stock builds in April will turn to draws in May through August and can push prices into \$90s in September. We opt to keep our price forecast unchanged (high \$80s/90 through May, \$85 thereafter) as OPEC<sup>’</sup>s rollover does not address 2025 imbalances. We maintain a bullish risk bias around our base-line price view but flag that if might shift back to neutral were the US-Saudi security deal to succeed (Oil Markets Weekly, May 2<sup>nd</sup>).

Agricultural and livestock indices have underperformed broad commodity indices through 2024 to-date. Our April 2024 global agri commodity balances show stocks-to-use ratios tightening across the board from 2023/24 through 2024/25 as weather related challenges and declining producer margins are weighing on output growth, amid firm demand. Rising availability across US markets has however pressured prices and we see scope for an increase in US export share over the year ahead amid declining inventories elsewhere. Our revised grain and oilseed price forecasts are constructive relative to spot levels and neutral relative to forward curves, while we are bullish Sugar and Cotton relative to spot levels and forwards. We hold a bullish price risk bias across the agri complex off current lows (Agricultural Markets Update, May 2<sup>nd</sup>).

Boosted central bank gold demand is structural, but pace of purchasing is still price sensitive. After net central bank gold purchases in 2022 and 2023, 2024 has started strongly again with net purchases of 290 tonnes in 1Q24. As central banks are buying more gold structurally, it also appears they are becoming a bit more tactical around price. With current gold prices about 10% higher than the 1Q24 average, this could act as a headwind to matching 1Q24<sup>’</sup>s strong pace of buying this quarter (Metals Weekly, May 3<sup>rd</sup>).

## EU: Parliamentary elections unlikely to move the needle

● Political right projected to increase vote share in June European Parliamentary elections

• Likely decreased focus on green agenda and more on competitiveness

Leveraging private capital and enhancing capital markets union becoming a key political imperative..

• ..But unlikely to impact near term macro unless US election magnifies geopolitical risks

Commission likely to be fully operational only by end-2024

From Thursday 6 June to Sunday 9 June, EU citizens will elect the European Parliament (EP) for the next five years. The results will impact the priorities and policy directions of the EU, including the composition of the leadership of the European Commission (the EU<sup>’</sup>s executive branch). The 720 Members (MEPs) are elected by the EU countries broadly according to their size; for example Germany will elect 96 MEPs and Malta will elect 6. In most countries, MEPs are elected through proportional representation from candidate lists put forward by national political parties. For this reason, the election may also be a test of public opinion for national politics. Once elected, MEPs align themselves with political groupings in the EP, although much of that has already been decided before the election and reflects which grouping their national party is a member of (Table 1).

Table 1: Political groupings for EP election
<table><tr><td>Name</td><td>Alignment</td><td>Lead Candidate</td></tr><tr><td>EPP - European People&#x27;s Party</td><td>Centre-right</td><td>von der Leyen</td></tr><tr><td>S&amp;D - Alliance of Socialists, Democrats</td><td>Centre-left</td><td>Schmitt</td></tr><tr><td>Renew Europe</td><td>Liberal</td><td>Strack-Zimmermann, Gozi</td></tr><tr><td>Greens/EFA - Greens/European</td><td>Left</td><td>Eikhout, Reintke,</td></tr><tr><td>Free Alliance ECR - European</td><td></td><td>Rossberg, Romeva</td></tr><tr><td>Conservatives/Reformists</td><td>Right/Eurosceptic</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>ID - Identity and Democracy</td><td>Right/Eurosceptic</td><td>Vistisen</td></tr><tr><td>The Left</td><td>Left</td><td>Baier</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan

According to the EP website, the new MEPs will elect during a first plenary session a new President of the EP, whose role is to preside over the debates and activities of the EP and to sign off on new laws and the EU budget. The President of the EP is normally elected for 2.5 years, so that a second president is elected half-way through the term of the EP. More consequentially, at a subsequent plenary session, the new MEPs elect a new President of the European Commission and later examine and approve the entire College of Commissioners, i.e. the leaders of the various policy areas of the Commission.

In practice, the process is more complex, especially the election of the Commission President. The Commission President has in the past been proposed by the European Council, which consists of the heads of state and government. However, ahead of the elections, parties can nominate a “Spitzenkan didat<sup>”</sup>. There has been talk of the “Spitzenkandidat<sup>”</sup> of the party with the most votes being automatically accepted as Commission President. In 2019, however, the leader of the winning EPP grouping, Manfred Weber, failed to secure broader support and therefore the European Council was able to propose an alternative candidate, Ursula von der Leyen.

In terms of timeline, after the 2019 election, the EP elected a new president in six weeks . At the same time, the European Council proposed Ursula von der Leyen as new Commission president and two weeks later the EP voted to approve her nomination. Hence, it took eight weeks for a Commission President to be confirmed. The process of picking and confirming all Commissioners took six months, constraining policy-making during that period.

## A further move to the right likely

The centre-right EPP grouping is likely to secure the most seats, losing only a few seats from 2019 (Figure 1). Other centrist groupings are also likely to lose some votes, as are the greens (left), with the two right/Eurosceptic ECR and ID groupings picking up support. The combined seats of the right wing parties will still likely be below that of the EPP, and hence far below the collective centrist vote. And they also should not be seen as a common block. For example, Meloni<sup>’</sup>s Brothers of Italy party is in ECR while Salvini<sup>’</sup>s Lega and Germany<sup>’</sup>s AfD are in ID.

Figure 1: EP election seat predictions
![](images/1473a5b76de6da85d96992ef8d6c59bcef8d291775aed79cfb333aea6e27526d.jpg)

However, von der Leyen<sup>’</sup>s path to Commission President is not certain. In 2019, she received 383 vote in the EP with 374 needed, even though the political groupings supporting her had 444 seats. She is also unlikely to receive all centrist votes this time round. At this stage, she is the most likely Commission President, but other names are being mentioned in case she falls short, including Mario Draghi.

## Election process and timetable is drawn out

Following nominations by member state governments, the new President of the European Commission will be elected in the inaugural session of the new European Parliament, which should take place in mid-July. Then, during the next few months, national governments will put forward their candidates for Commissioners (the EU equivalent of ministers at the country level). The candidates need to be approved by the Parliament, following which the new Commission takes office and publishes an official 5-year work program. Given the various steps, and the 2019 experience, this will likely only happen in late 2024.

## No clear implications for macro

The apparent shift to the right in the polls so far continues trends seen in recent state elections in Germany and national elections in the Netherlands, Portugal, and Spain. Unsurprisingly, this has already impacted the work of the Commission and the EU agenda in general: while the green transition remains important, more focus is now being put on competitiveness-related issues.

Geopolitical developments elsewhere are also adding to the challenges facing the EU. These include the war in Ukraine, trade tensions with China, the US inflation Reduction Act (IRA), and the possibility of a Trump victory. The IRA has certainly increased concerns about competitiveness amongst European policymakers, as has the rise of Chinese manufacturers of EVs and solar panels. A response is complicated by less fiscal capacity at the EU level compared to the federal level in the US, and the restrictions imposed by EU state aid rules.

Overall, however, we don<sup>’</sup>t see major near-term macro implications emanating from the EP elections unless the November US election result leads to a major increase in geopolitical risks. The competitiveness agenda; especially the need to leverage private capital for the green and digital transitions, AI, and defence spending; is already materially increasing the focus on capital markets union (CMU). Recently published reports by Enrica Letta and the French Treasury have detailed proposals to enhance the attractiveness of European capital markets (e.g., on securitization, development of European long-term savings products, and EU level supervision). But the disagreements amongst member states that have stymied progress before are still very prevalent (e.g., on harmonization of insolvency regimes and withholding taxes). So rapid

progress will likely be hard to deliver, especially without further progress on Banking Union given the centrality of banks to financial intermediation in the EU. Mario Draghi is expected to publish a report on how to enhance EU competitiveness around end-June, and is likely to repeat the calls for further progress on CMU and more EU-level fiscal instruments. How to reform state aid rules to address the current challenges posed by US and China will also be an important issue.

Defence spending is increasing across the EU, but further use of EU level funding/instruments could well depend on the results of the US election. The struggle to get NGEU over the line, and the lack of additional EU instruments that accompanied the temporary relaxation of state aid rules, goes to show how hard it is to agree on new EU-level instruments. Europe often requires a crisis for major changes, and a Trump victory could well provide that if it leads to a rapid reduction in US military support for the war in Ukraine. That could provide the impetus for a more serious discussion about EU level funding for defence (rather than it happening mainly at the national level).

The new EU fiscal rules should enter into force over the next month or so. They have been approved by the EP and are now just awaiting the final sign-off by EU Ministers. Thus the elections themselves should not have any impact on the new rules, although how to make any substantial increase in defence spending square with them is an open question. There does appear to be some wiggle room in the new rules, however, given additional defence spending will be considered as one of the factors impacting the assessments of compliance.

Migration and EU enlargement will also be key focus points for the next EP. Given the likely shift towards the right, migration might become even more of a politically sensitive issue. But as labour markets across the EU still remain tight, “real politik<sup>”</sup> may mean continued high levels of net migration to alleviate skill shortages.

## ECB challenged by inflation data, less so by the Fed

• We modestly change our ECB call, removing an October cut, and slowing next year’s cuts

• Fed policy matters for the ECB, but not mechanically; FX and market spillovers are modest so far

• Ultimately, domestic data will determine ECB policy and they are posing some challenge

Euro area inflation still has a decent chance of falling to 2%, but the path remains uncertain

Firm US inflation and growth data have led markets to pare back their expectations for Fed rate cuts. At the same time, the ECB has dug in over a June cut. This shows that there are no mechanical links between Fed and ECB policy. But, policy divergences still matter for the ECB. The main consideration will be whether incoming news on US growth and inflation gives a broader signal for the Euro area outlook, how strong any macro spillovers from US growth are, and whether there are spillovers via financial markets, including via the currency. Via these channels, the ECB will assess whether US developments affect its staff<sup>’</sup>s forecasts for Euro area inflation.

Financial market spillovers can be significant and the impact of a large currency depreciation on Euro area inflation is significant. But, the linkages are also far from straightforward and depend a lot on the context. As a result, the ECB is likely to be agnostic and adjust its policy plans only if it sees evidence of significant market moves. At the same time, the domestic data are providing the main challenge at present: growth is improving without clear signs of improving productivity growth and services inflation is remaining sticky. We still see Euro area inflation eventually falling to 2% without the labor market having to weaken, but visibility over this path remains low. To better balance risks, we take out an October cut from our forecast, taking this year's easing to 75bp and also modestly slow next year<sup>’</sup>s cuts, but without currently changing the endpoint of a 2% policy rate.

## FX spillover likely modest for inflation

A common question is whether Fed policy will act as a constraint on the ECB, with a key focus on the currency channel. All else equal, higher Fed versus ECB policy rates should put downward pressure on the exchange rate and upward pressure on Euro area inflation, mainly via energy prices. The ECB<sup>’</sup>s main macro models (Hahn, NAWM and NMCM) have relatively large effects from a 1% trade-weighted currency change on Euro area inflation of up to 0.1%-pt per year for up to three years (Table 1). A newer estimate from an ECB

Occasional Paper (Communale and Kunovac) estimated a smaller effect, with headline inflation affected by 0.11%-pt in Year 1 and barely impacted in Years 2 and 3.

Table 1: Exchange rate passthrough to Euro area inflation %-pt impact of a 1% NEER depreciation
<table><tr><td></td><td>Year 1</td><td>Year 2 and 3 avg.</td><td>Total</td></tr><tr><td>Hahn (2023)</td><td>0.10</td><td>0.07</td><td>0.23</td></tr><tr><td>New area-wide model</td><td>0.02</td><td>0.09</td><td>0.20</td></tr><tr><td>New multi-country model</td><td>0.09</td><td>0.08</td><td>0.25</td></tr><tr><td>Communale and Kunovac</td><td>0.11</td><td>0.01</td><td>0.12</td></tr></table>

Source: ECB and J.P. Morgan

A few other messages are sent by ECB research. First, the dollar has an amplified impact when compared to other currencies as it is the invoicing currency of commodities. But, even dollar moves need to be permanent and must correlate with other currency moves to have the effects in Table 1. Second, the extent of the passthrough may have declined over time. For example, a rolling estimate of the model in Hahn (2023) shows a decline of the total passthrough effect from 0.23%-pt to almost zero into the run-up to the pandemic. This may reflect structural factors and increased monetary policy credibility. Third, the actual passthrough depends a lot on the nature and cause of the currency shock, for example whether it reflects a domestic supply shock, a global demand shock, and domestic versus foreign monetary policy. A currency change that comes alongside stronger growth will likely have a larger inflation effect. Fourth, most estimates are for headline inflation (as in Table 1). For core, the ECB has reported some results for core goods, which are very modest: Figure 1 shows a 0.2-0.3%-pt peak impact from a 10%, not 1%, appreciation. The impact on services is likely also small.

Figure 1: Effect of a 10% currency appreciation on core goods infl. %-pt on inflation rate, ECB estimates
![](images/4c75b6583d5c91504ddf3e85c589e88ac560c1b584ed50e6105f32edaab48abe.jpg)

Ultimately, the currency effect of a Fed-ECB divergence depends on the actual moves in markets. So far, the Euro looks relatively range-bound against the dollar and has actually appreciated in trade-weighted terms (Figure 2). According to our FX strategists, divergences in the policy rate outlook have played a role recently, but improvements in Euro area growth data should support the Euro (Figure 3). This highlights that many factors matter for the exchange rate, not just the interest rate differential to the Fed.

Figure 2: Euro effective exchange rate
![](images/50f782e75674e796432f23f14ef98a52d810505d7005bf532222dcc31470988c.jpg)

Figure 3: Euro area and US forward money market rates
![](images/eb989cc8d049230b2fd8b288f23542d1a8f7e4ae2ce4429d30c6eb62a64d9d9f.jpg)

## Possible spillover via longer-term rates

Our colleagues in Rates Strategy note that spillovers via forward rates are weaker when differences in macro conditions justify a divergence in policy rates. In 2017-18, Fed hikes had no impact on Euro area forward rates given that Euro area inflation was stuck near 1% (Figure 4). Correlations have been higher over the past 2-3 years due to more common inflation drivers. Hence, what incoming data reveal about the US and Euro area inflation processes will be key for spillovers via forward rates. Also, it will matter if delayed Fed cuts reflect slower disinflation or the need for a larger labor market correction. For now, the ECB is expressing confidence about Euro area disinflation, which suggests that it will only adjusts its plans if the currency or longer-term rates materially move. A pre-emptive response seems less likely at this stage.

Figure 4: Euro area and US forward money market rates % p.a., 1y1y forward
![](images/eb587d063ac688c9d6810710e7cca4e807cfe1841f294677e1f85daecb6bcff9.jpg)

## Challenges from the domestic data

In our view, the bigger challenge for the ECB is not the recent changes in Fed expectations, but the incoming Euro area inflation data. The ECB seems set on delivering a June cut, with data apparently only affecting subsequent decisions. But, those subsequent decisions are facing a challenge from the data.

Our forecast continues to make the key judgment that Euro area inflation can ultimately fall back to 2% without the labor market having to weaken. A few considerations underpin this. First, the 1% inflation regime before the pandemic gives some room to accommodate permanent changes to wage and price setting and a lower unemployment rate. Second, we still assume that high inflation has been a key driver of the pickup in wage growth and that this effect will eventually wane as headline inflation has already declined to close to 2%. Third, there may still be some energy and (possibly) pandemic effects that can drop out of the core inflation data. Some noisy items have also affected the most recent inflation data. Due to these judgments, we still assume that inflation can sustainably slow to 2% and that the ECB can cut rates back to neutral, which we assume is 2%.

Of course, there are real uncertainties around all of these judgments. Importantly, some of the judgments above are about assumed magnitudes of structural changes, which are hard to make. As a result, it is crucial to track this in the data. Recently, progress has looked mixed at best. First, services inflation continues to look sticky, with recent data showing momentum at above a 4%ar (Figure 5). Certainly, this is creating upside risk to the ECB staff<sup>’</sup>s 2Q24 core inflation forecast. But, some of the momentum may also relate to start-ofthe-year price resetting (incl. in insurance) and the German VAT hike on restaurant meals.

Figure 5: Euro area services inflation %oya and %3m saar
![](images/5bf80fc6b44d67528fc8fc203d71971ec805a2ec135180740e354d53ea008bbc.jpg)

Second, the pickup in GDP growth is not leading to a clear pickup in productivity, which is still biasing the unemployment rate lower and unit labor cost growth higher. Wage growth is also still firm, although some of that likely still reflects payments to compensate for the past inflation surge. Third, one area where the data have moved in a more constructive direction is the GDP deflator, which is slowing and hinting at unit profits exerting less upward pressure on domestic price pressures (Figure 6). But, even this indicator has to slow further and is being propped up for now by the dynamics on unit labor costs.

Figure 6: Euro area EC price expectation survey and GDP deflator %bal
![](images/07c4f5f31f28de9033c866dd21220ae353ed594d15240a449558df1f1bc9a97f.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The slow disinflation progress so far is not yet invalidating the key call that Euro area inflation can eventually slow back to the 2% target without the labor market having to weaken. But, the data clearly suggest that the path to that endpoint remains quite uncertain. Even though the ECB has seemingly already decided to cut in June, despite key data challenging the staff<sup>’</sup>s near-term forecast, Governing Council members are expressing more caution about the pace of subsequent cuts. Our impression from recent commentary is that most Governing Council members currently see 2-3 rate cuts this year. We therefore remove the October cut from our forecast, leaving a total easing of 75bp this year, delivered at the meetings in June, September and December. This effectively takes our forecast back to the one we laid out in our 2024 Outlook. That growth is improving should give the ECB the space to go slower this year, even though it has been expecting a pick-

Over time, we assume that the data will deliver clearer signs of inflation slowing towards the 2% target. But, as this may take time and happen against a firmer growth backdrop, we also slow next year<sup>’</sup>s cuts by delaying the January cut. This means that a 2% policy rate is reached in September rather than June next year. All of this is still a long time away and there are many factors that can influence that endpoint.

## BoE: The challenge of sustaining inflation at 2%

Inflation is set to drop back close to target this spring, but keeping it there will be a challenge

Even assuming a tepid pace of goods inflation, the MPC probably needs to see services return to 2.5%

That’s a long way down from the current 5% pace, and may be hard to achieve absent a significant shock

Its never really possible to sustain inflation at the target, as there are always shocks hitting the economy. But the MPC has managed to do so on average, at least up until the pandemic. It has, however, often benefitted from a combination of falling import prices and labour market slack. Even with pandemic shocks in reverse, the challenge ahead looks daunting. Globally determined goods prices seem unlikely to fall back into deflation. Services inflation looks sticky at around 5%, but we estimate the BoE will need to engineer a slowing to around 2.5% as seen just before the the pandemic. The labour market has loosened and second round effects from the fall in energy prices are still working through. But wage and pipeline services inflation are still running way above prepandemic levels with limited signs of progress. With GDP growth now picking up, the BoE may well struggle to maintain inflation at the target without a positive supply shock or a negative demand shock.

## Good luck vs. good policy

Since the MPC began targeting the CPI in late 2003, inflation has averaged just a little above 2%. This was likely aided by the BoE<sup>’</sup>s independent control of monetary policy and an explicit inflation target, which helped stabilize inflation expectations. But viewed from a different perspective, the MPC has benefited from some helping hands that may not be around in the future.

As illustrated in Table 1, core goods prices were in deflation from 2004-2007, enabling services inflation to run hot at 3.6%. Rare currency strength and the integration of low cost goods producers into the global trading system enabled the MPC to deliver inflation close to target during this period. When goods inflation turned positive after the financial crisis, the MPC struggled as services inflation remained strong and the CPI averaged 3.1% from 2008 to 2013. The 2014-2019 period marked something of a sweet spot, as services inflation slowed materially. But this followed a long period of high unemployment from a weak post-GFC recovery, and included a period of falling global goods prices that left inflation undershooting the target. A unique and synchronized set of price shocks delivered unusually high inflation during the

post pandemic 2020-2023 period. But the latest data for early 2024 show inflation remaining stubbornly high despite the fall in goods price gains, owing to elevated services inflation.

Table 1: A short history of CPI inflation targetting %
<table><tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td rowspan="2">CPI</td><td rowspan="2">Wedge</td><td colspan="3">Core</td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>Goods</td><td>Services</td></tr><tr><td>Pre-pandemic</td><td>2.2</td><td>0.4</td><td>1.9</td><td>0.0</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>2004-2007</td><td>2.2</td><td>0.7</td><td>1.5</td><td>-1.5</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>2008-2013</td><td>3.1</td><td>0.8</td><td>2.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>3.5</td></tr><tr><td>2014-2019</td><td>1.4</td><td>-0.2</td><td>1.7</td><td>0.5</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Post-pandemic</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>2020-2023</td><td>5.1</td><td>0.8</td><td>4.3</td><td>3.8</td><td>4.5</td></tr><tr><td>Latest</td><td>3.2</td><td>-1.0</td><td>4.2</td><td>1.5</td><td>6.0</td></tr><tr><td>Simulation</td><td>2.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>1.9</td><td>0.8</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Weight</td><td>1.00</td><td>0.22</td><td>0.78</td><td>0.29</td><td>0.49</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan. 1. %-pt gap between headline and core inflation

At the end of Table 1 we include a simulation that assumes core goods inflation runs at 0.8% while food and energy contribute a tenth to CPI inflation. We don<sup>’</sup>t take a very specific view on how events will play out, but a repeat of the sustained core goods deflation seen a couple of decades ago seems unlikely. While China is exporting deflation due to excess capacity, other factors such as climate objectives, geopolitical risks and lessons from the pandemic create more pressure for onshoring and locally sourced goods that may act to unwind some of the efficiency gains from globalization. The UK also has relatively weak trend growth which will likely weigh on the currency through time as interest rates normalize.

If this broad perspective is correct, the BoE would need to engineer a decline in services inflation from its current 6% year ago rate to within a 2-3% range to have any chance of meeting the inflation target. This assumes the weight of services and other CPI components remains close to current levels and that the wedge between headline and core inflation is less than half its 2003-2019 average. Our simulation shows that services inflation would need to run at 2.5% to meet the inflation target given these assumptions. If core goods were to instead run flat, services inflation could be higher at 3%. But on the other hand a higher 1.5% pace of core goods inflation would require services down at 2%. In either case that<sup>’</sup>s a long way to travel.

## Stubborn services inflation

Services inflation has been boosted both by a tight labour market and second round effects from strong imported and energy price pressures. This year there has been significant progress on both fronts. Both the vacancy to unemployed ratio - a measure of tightness in the labour market - and headline inflation have moved back close to pre-pandemic levels.

This has created an expectation that domestic inflation should show a clear slowing. There is indeed evidence of this happening, with both wage growth and services inflation down from their highs (Figure 1). But the process has been slow, and there is a debate about why such a delay is occurring.

Figure 1: Labour market tightness and inflation
![](images/ac9890c2387af344a084f981c2a9a3275caf83e9836852ec31cb11723855a229.jpg)

The dovish view is that domestic inflation exhibits significant persistence and will drop back to pre-pandemic levels with a long lag. But the challenge to this view is that the V/U ratio is still high compared to its historical average of 0.4. Moreover, wage inflation appears to be behaving very differently way from its past relationship with the V/U ratio. This could reflect a decline in matching efficiency, such that a given pool of unemployed people are now less well suited to a given level of vacancies.

We argued last year that there was some evidence of this, following a sustained decline in the job finding probability after the 2016 EU referendum and the associated collapse in EU work related immigration. We cannot update this analysis because the details of the household Labour Force Survey have been temporarily suspended. But it may explain some of the stickiness in underlying inflation. Many place significant weight on the drop in headline as a sign that domestic inflation will be brought under control. But there may be more friction in the labor market than implied by the V/U ratio, which would imply more persistent price pressures.

## Best of luck

Even though goods have a higher energy content, services still use energy and hence inflation in the sector might not necessarily be a clean barometer of underlying persistence. Some cite this argument as a reason for expecting a further significant slowing in services inflation as the lags associated with the past decline in gas prices fade. Energy intensive services sectors - as defined by Megan Greene in her November speech - have indeed shown a sharp slowing since early 2023 as would have been expected. That process looks set to continue. But it is curious just how little progress has been made on non-energy services inflation, which should be a more reliable measure of the underlying process (Figure 2). This measure remains extraordinarily high, with no clear signs of a trend lower.

Figure 2: Services inflation
![](images/73f2e81df887310d429996c392b8c0d8a21a3b569f1070d3f5631b8edc99138a.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan calculations

We expect both these measures to show some slowing from here: the run rate on the non-energy component has already downshifted to a 5% pace, and wage growth is also down from its highs. But the stickiness of the non-energy component should be a red flag that something is not working as expected. Tracking the high frequency changes in these and other underlying metrics is key for understanding where the inflation process might be heading, and prompted us recently to publish a heatmap drawing attention to these variables.

One dovish view from the MPC comes from Swati Dhingra, who has argued that high levels of pay growth are a lagging indicator of past inflation whereas the PPI contains more useful leading properties. It<sup>’</sup>s not immediately clear why this should be the case, as the causality between wages and business prices likely runs in both directions. In any case, its noteworthy that the run rate on the services PPI through to 1Q has started to accelerate again. This is preventing the year ago rate from extending the decline seen in the year to 3Q23, which is starting to look more like an energy story (as in Figure 2). Like wage growth, services PPI inflation is still running significantly above its pre-pandemic pace (Figure 3). If the goal is to return back to that period, the journey looks long and without clear signs the process is on track.

Figure 3: PPI services and average earnings wage growth
![](images/fd98da1a4887f42d585c07f5439db7e9019ea2a08eb3cfa5dbaf3fd8e01a9fab.jpg)

## Egypt: Back from the brink

• Near-term financing pressures have abated following the US\$35bn UAE land deal

• We now expect US\$16.2bn FX reserve accretion in FY24 and US\$2.6bn in FY25

Headline inflation should slow in 2H, but monetary policy will remain tighter for longer...

• … while medium-term fiscal and external sustainability hinges on sustaining current structural reforms

Barely three months ago, the Egyptian macro story was of FX distortions amplifying imbalances amid loose monetary policy and high inflation. The IMF programme engaged in 2022 had stalled, largely on the reluctance to enact the broad ranging reforms prescribed by the IMF, while authorities mostly resorted to capital restrictions to limit FX reserve pressures. However, a landmark US\$35bn UAE land deal, along with monetary, fiscal and FX adjustments have completely changed the near-term financing picture and outlook.

The IMF along with the World Bank and the European Commission have pledged support for authorities' reform programme over the next three years. Likewise, foreign portfolio flows have resumed, attracted to the carry trade following the positive outlook and as near-term financing pressures appear to have eased. Here we detail the recent changes and examine the path ahead for monetary and fiscal policy, as well as considering external and debt sustainability.

## A sizable upsize

The upsized IMF programme (to US\$8bn from US\$3bn) remains anchored on a policy mix centred on a liberalised foreign exchange market, significant monetary and fiscal tightening, and increased private sector activity to drive growth. However, the IMF has added two more quantitative performance criteria (QPC) along with two additional indicative targets (Table 1). The new QPCs put a cap on monetary financing of fiscal authorities including other public entities. A public investment ceiling has also been added as an indicative target. The third review of the programme is scheduled for June 2024, for which end-Mar targets will assessed. After that the IMF will return to its usual bi-annual reviews. We think authorities are already likely to have met all end-Mar QPCs and indicative targets.

Table 1: IMF programme QPC and IT in EGP bn (unless otherwise indicated)

## Near-term financing pressures have eased

We now expect FX reserve accretion of US\$16.2bn in FY24 and US\$2.6bn in FY25. This contrasts with IMF<sup>’</sup>s expectation which only sees a US\$12bn FX reserves increase this year and US\$3bn in FY25. For both years, the variation (vs IMF) is largely on current account expectations. While we expect the CAD to widen to US\$15.2bn (4% of GDP) this year, the IMF projection of US\$21.8bn (6.3% of GDP) is a near record high on the back of forecasts for higher imports and primary income outflows. For FY25, the IMF has not taken into account the expected development project flows linked to the Ras El Hekma (REH) land deal, hence its CAD and FDI expectations are more benign compared to our projections (Table 2). Furthermore, the IMF expects only US\$400mn of net foreign portfolio investment flows, compared to our expectation of US\$8.1bn, which might actually be conservative considering the large net inflows into the domestic market. The local exchange EGX reported \$14bn of foreign inflows into domestic securities between March and April.

Recent FX inflows already helped banks to improve their NFA positions - a phenomenon that is currently only marginally built into the current IMF forecast. End of March data already show a massive US\$25bn improvement in overall banking system NFA from its January trough (Figure 1). All told, the updated IMF net international reserve (NIR) targets of US\$20.5bn in Mar 24 and US\$30.3bn in Jun 24 appear to be easily achievable against the backdrop of the current and expected flows over the coming months. We think the current NIR targets are likely to be revised higher in the next programme reviews. This is as the IMF has NIR adjustors in the programme for which the floor of the NIR will be adjusted up by 50% of any excess in the stock of non residents holdings of domestic bills and bonds relative to programme assumptions. Authorities expect to receive an additional US\$14bn in new inflows from the UAE for the completion of the REH land deal (plus US\$6bn conversion of UAE deposit at Central Bank of Egypt) while IMF board approval in June should see another US\$820mn disbursed.
