## Disinflation on course but risks remain

Our forecasts is for disinflation that leads headline inflation towards 22%oya by December, from 33% in March (Figure 3). Large base effects will support the inflation downtrend, but we also expect sequential momentum to remain benign barring any shocks from food prices and renewed FX pressures.

Figure 3: Egypt CPI headline and CBE O/N deposit rate
![](images/5d5d83aa894cc4538499113fabfde738e37ae33f8c60cb9ed5f924c6120e8ad2.jpg)

Further adjustments to fuel prices in a bid to further reduce the relative subsidy bill (1% of GDP), could also pose upside risks to the inflation outlook as local pump prices in Egypt remain well below the global average (Figure 4). As fuel accounts for about 2% of the inflation basket, the potential second-round effects could be more impactful on the headline number than direct effect. Risks might also arise from utilities, which account for a larger 3.7% of the CPI basket. Despite the slower inflation path we project, we still don<sup>’</sup>t see scope for monetary easing this year as we think the CBE needs to maintain a tight monetary policy stance to anchor inflation expectations and hold off any potential currency pressures.

Figure 4: Egypt fuel and utility prices
![](images/6f9e46e08418bfe2245a7fd2ed9a8d8686594831b4d8cdc06508746f7e4801d7.jpg)

We now have much less conviction about the additional 200bp hike we forecast for the May meeting, as the March inflation print surprised to the downside after the big jump recorded in February. Huge foreign portfolio inflows recorded in the last two months have not only helped anchor the currency but also forced a rally in Treasury bill yields. With the expectation that authorities will reduce domestic issuance this quarter, we think scope for further rate hikes is likely now limited. Our Taylor rule model also echoes those thoughts particularly with the recent vast improvement in the banking system NFA position. Running an alternative scenario where the US FED does not cut rates, the Taylor rule suggests rates should be 135bp higher than the current baseline scenario (where JPM expects 125bp in cuts by June 2025 - Figure 5). We will reassess our policy rate forecast after the April inflation print as inflation dynamics have been volatile of late.

Figure 5: CBE deposit rate and Taylor-rule style mode
![](images/3909aeda630554dd5a5b4ed045702aeb1e3928048e3ffce2a31ff53732a08d43.jpg)

## Ambitious fiscal consolidation path

The authorities are targeting strong fiscal consolidation for FY25 and subsequent years. Egypt<sup>’</sup>s fiscal position has been under pressure due to its high interest expenditures, despite consistent primary surpluses in recent years. Proceeds from the REH deal provided a much needed source of relief, increasing revenues for about 4% of GDP in FY24. However, due to the large concentration of short-term domestic debt and tighter monetary policy, interest expenditures are expected to rise to 9.7% of GDP in FY24 and 11% in FY25, while also accounting for c.70% of revenues. Authorities are now seek ing to start extending duration as they have recently agreed with pension funds, insurance companies and banks to start rolling over short-term debt maturities to longer-dated bonds. Over the medium term, the authorities hope to increase the average term to maturity on debt closer to five years from the current 3.3 years. The expected drop in inflation over the next couple of years should ensure cheaper debt issuance.

Table 3: Egypt draft budget FY25 - EGP tn
<table><tr><td></td><td>Central govt budget (gross)</td><td>Consolidated budget*</td></tr><tr><td>Balance</td><td>-1.24</td><td>-1.33</td></tr><tr><td>% of GDP</td><td>-7.3</td><td>-7.8</td></tr><tr><td>Primary balance</td><td>0.59</td><td>0.61</td></tr><tr><td>% of GDP</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Revenues</td><td>2.63</td><td>5.30</td></tr><tr><td>Tax</td><td>2.02</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Non-tax</td><td>0.60</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Expenditures</td><td>3.87</td><td>6.63</td></tr><tr><td>o/w Interest</td><td>1.83</td><td>1.95</td></tr></table>

Source: MoF, J.P. Morgan \*including other public bodies

More importantly, the IMF expects the authorities to persistently lower gross budget sector debt from an estimated 96% of GDP in FY24 to 61% in FY29. Such an aggressive debt reduction path will require much larger primary surpluses, consistently north of 5% of GDP from FY26. Despite the increase in tax revenues expected from VAT reforms and removal of tax exemptions, we still think that the debt reduction path over the next five years is ambitious, all the more so as it excludes divestment proceeds. However, we do expect that public debt to GDP will take a significant leg down to 85% in FY25 on the back of higher nominal growth and a

larger primary surplus (Table 3). The larger primary surplus expectation is important for the IMF<sup>’</sup>s debt sustainablity assessment. Although Egypt<sup>’</sup>s debt is currently assessed to be sustainable, it is not with a high probability and also comes with a high risk of sovereign stress (Figure 6).

Figure 6: IMF programme debt and primary balance projections
![](images/da3915daab33971594fc7a3260a3af40cdb1143bc52159acb4f135f3501f005a.jpg)
Source: IMF, J.P. Morgan

## Positive growth outlook despite fiscal consolidation

Fiscal consolidation will have a large effect on fiscal thrust, but the normalisation of economic activity and REH development flows could boost growth. The primary balance in FY24 was estimated at over 5% of GDP from 1.6%. However, when the one-off of REH is removed, the picture changed quite drastically with a primary broadly in line with the previous year. Hence, a sharp change on structural balance will largely happen in FY25 with our estimate of fiscal thrust strongly negative, taking out 2%pt from GDP growth. Despite this, and a tighter monetary policy, we remain positive on growth as we expect a bounce back to 4.3% in FY25 from our estimate of 2.8% in FY23. The end of a dual exchange rate system and a normalisation of imports and flows of capital should benefit economic activity. Furthermore, the start of development works in Ras El-Hekma could give a further boost in the second half of FY25. In this review, the IMF did not assess the potential impact of REH on economic activity and the balance of payments as yet. Hence, the IMF projection of 4.4% growth in FY25 appears more bullish than ours.

Figure 7: Egypt fiscal thrust
![](images/ce869586e81c635bc03d144b6e421b317616014add0f9880274cd63931438a60.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan - estimates for fiscal year

## China: The future of the housing market

• China<sup>’</sup>s housing market correction is not over yet...

• …based on both an international comparison and the current market situation

• The solution lies in public and rental housing

• Key measures: institutionalized funding support in the near term, rural land reform in the long run

Since 2021, China<sup>’</sup> s housing market has experienced the most severe downturn since the reforms allowed the development of the private housing market (known as commodity housing) in the late 1990s (Figure 1). As of 1Q24, new home sales fell by 46% (in value terms) or 48% (in floor space) from the peak in 2021. New home starts (in floor space) fell by 60%, and the share of real estate investment as % of GDP fell from 14.0% in 2020 to 8.4% in 1Q24. The collapse in housing market activity not only drags on economic growth, but also increases financial vulnerabilities related to local government hidden debt, shadow credit and regional banks.

Figure 1: Key indicators in China's housing market (12m rolling sum)
![](images/a002f52d3d9a512da8825959890576b359919eb9d91633e497caecc2d24967a6.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

## A cross-country comparison: China vs. international experiences

How severe is China<sup>’</sup>s housing market correction? Here we assess price and volume indicators in an international comparison.

In term of house price decline, China<sup>’</sup>s new home price declined by 5-6% and secondary home price fell slightly over 10% from the peak in 2021, with aggregate home price down by about 10% (Figure 2). Compared to a few well-known house price correction episodes that have been popularly cited in the comparison, China<sup>’</sup>s house price decline is modest. It is similar to Korea<sup>’</sup>s episode in 2Q91-3Q95, but much less severe than HK (3Q97-3Q03), Japan (1Q91-2Q09), the US (1Q06-1Q11) and Spain (3Q07-1Q04) (Figure 3 and Table 1).

Figure 2: China's house price Index, 3Q21=100
![](images/7a7c42023156ccde875956f340986bc236cfacbfea24d04c59ce3bee0edb2ac6.jpg)
Source: BIS, NBS, J.P. Morgan

Figure 3: Nominal home price
![](images/611fd8c1f1010c39b4134b7be891cc30f7f2b40df990f1d1a2d5299487eca887.jpg)
Source: BIS, J.P. Morgan.
Note: Japan's house price decline lasted 73 quarters with cumulative decline of 46.5%.

Two things are worth noting. First, the benchmark is important. The chosen episodes are among the most severe housing market corrections in modern history. Table 1 summarizes 90 house price decline episodes in 57 economies since 1970 (based on BIS data). The average duration of the housing downturns is 10 quarters and the average magnitude of house price decline is 17%. China<sup>’</sup>s house price has declined for 10 quarters (sample average) with a cumulative decline of 10% (around 40 percentile in the global sample). The international experience has been quite divergent, both in duration (minimum 1 quarter in Russia starting in 4Q10 and maximum 73 quarters in Japan starting in 1Q91) and the magnitude (minimum -3% in Luxembourg in 2Q08-3Q09 and maximum -65% in HK in 3Q97-3Q03). Among the above chosen episodes, except for Korea, the other four episodes rank among the top-10 percentile in terms of duration, and the top 20-percentile in terms of the magnitude of house price decline.

In addition, the relatively modest decline in house prices in China reflects stronger price control by the government, e.g., a ceiling control on home price inflation during upturns and a floor control during downturns (especially new home prices). The control on new home prices runs at the cost of a larger volume correction, perhaps an intentionally chosen strategy to mitigate financial spillover risk (link). As a result, in volume terms, China<sup>’</sup>s housing market correction has been remarkable. New home starts in China have declined by 60% from the peak, more severe than Japan and the US but less than

Spain (Figure 4).

Table 1: International house price decline episodes
<table><tr><td>Duration (quarters)</td><td>HP decline (%)</td></tr><tr><td>Full sample (1970-2023)</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Average</td><td>10 -17</td></tr><tr><td>Maximum</td><td>73 -65</td></tr><tr><td>75-percentile</td><td>13 -24</td></tr><tr><td>Median</td><td>8 -12</td></tr><tr><td>25-percentile</td><td>4 -8</td></tr><tr><td>Minimum</td><td>1 -3</td></tr><tr><td>Selected episodes</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>China (3Q21-1Q24)</td><td>10 -10</td></tr><tr><td>HK (3Q97-3Q03)</td><td>24 -65</td></tr><tr><td>Japan (1Q91-2Q09)</td><td>73 -46.5</td></tr><tr><td>Korea (2Q91-3Q95)</td><td>17 -13.4</td></tr><tr><td>Spain (3Q07-1Q14)</td><td>26 -36.9</td></tr><tr><td>US (1Q06-1Q11)</td><td>20 -31.3</td></tr></table>

Source: BIS, J.P. Morgan. Our sample includes 90 house price decline episodes in 57 economies since 1970 based on BIS database.

Figure 4: New home starts
![](images/40b9159e56f031e93712cdf69d5416c97d46ba8a20f29b217df6d5ffd53ef26b.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

The asymmetric adjustment in home prices and housing activity volumes partly explains why the housing market correction has become a significant macro drag, but financial spillover risk has been contained in China (as house price decline is relatively modest and the implicit government protection on LGFV debt is strong). Real estate investment declined by 10% in both 2022 and 2023, dragging GDP growth down by roughly 1.5%pts in each year based on our estimates. The share of real estate and construction in GDP fell from 14.4% in 2020 to 12.6% in 2023 (Figure 5). What is somewhat unexpected is that, from an international comparison perspective, the share of real estate and construction in China is smaller than Japan and Spain even during China<sup>’</sup>s peak years, though it is higher than Korea and HK. In addition, the decline in the share (1.7%-pts in China) is larger than the Korea episode (-0.7%-pt) but more modest than Japan (-3.2%-pts), Spain (-3.4%-pts) and the US (-2.2%-pts).

Figure 5: Real estate and construction
![](images/e89ca9d09ea2d8d3150ea141826f90b8f34e21ba07e9352ff6bc80f6aeedf550.jpg)

Overall, international comparisons suggests that China<sup>’</sup>s housing market correction is still more modest than some more severe episodes. If one thinks China<sup>’</sup>s housing market problem (e.g., over-heating and over-valuation) was similar to these episodes, the correction process will likely continue. In particular, if the administrative control on new home prices were to be removed, house prices may decline further to clear the market.

## Market over-shooting will not end soon

Is China<sup>’</sup>s housing market correction approaching its end? Our property team estimates that the equilibrium demand growth in China<sup>’</sup>s housing market (based on estimates of home demand from urbanization, new marriages, re-settlements, home upgrade and investment demand) is currently about 1 billion square meters per annum. This implies that volume indicators (new home sales and new home starts) have entered an overshooting stage (see Figure 1). However, this does not necessarily mean that housing market activity will soon bottom out.

First, overshooting is a typical phenomenon in house market correction episodes. Housing market corrections took place because of excessive growth in earlier years. In China<sup>’</sup>s case, new home sales and new home starts were significantly above the equilibrium levels in 2018-22, leading to over-capacity in the housing market that will take time to absorb. For instance, new homes under construction are still above 5 billion sqm, much higher than the supply in a normal housing cycle (home construction equivalent to three years of demand). In addition, potential supply in the secondary home market will add further pressure. Despite the lack of official data on vacancy rates, some estimates suggest that the vacancy rate (owned but not occupied) may be around 20%. A reduction of 5-10% in vacancy rates would imply an additional 1.4-2.8 billion sqm supply from secondary home market.

Looking at near-term demand and supply conditions, both demand and supply likely will continue to be lower than the equilibrium value in the near term. On the demand side, weak income and house price expectations have added to weak

home demand. Meanwhile, concerns about home delivery have led to divergent performance in sales between SOE developers and private developers (Figure 6), and also generated divergence between pre-sales versus sales of completed projects or secondary home sales. On the supply side, weak home sales and liquidity pressure have caused developers to be more cautious in buying new land or starting new projects.

![](images/674a17b601acd9196e086f1665e47d5d311cc71d0982453e86b0fe6706d75a16.jpg)

Our forecast expects that, without a major change in housing policy, the housing market decline will continue in 2024, with key volume indicators (new home sales, new home starts, new home completion) falling another 10%. These volume activities will likely stabilize or bottom up in 2025, but the macro drag (decline in real estate investment and drag on growth) may persist for another 1-2 years.

We closely watch two indicators to assess the housing market outlook. One is house price dynamics. A narrowing in home price declines or price stabilization is positive for the recovery in home demand; by contrast, further (or faster) home price declines will lead to further downside risk to our baseline forecasts. The other is new home sales, especially for SOE developers. If home sales for SOE developers also deteriorate (Figure 6), they may face similar liquidity pressure to private developers (as home sales represent the most important funding source for real estate developers). Spillover from private to SOE developers would lead to further downside risk to our baseline forecasts.

## From housing.com to housing.org

With housing becoming the weakest link for the Chinese economy, policy has aimed to stabilize the housing market. So far, housing policy relaxation has mainly been city-specific and gradually phased in, hence not sufficient to achieve that aim.

The Politburo meeting on April 30 discussed the housing policy from the perspective of risk prevention. It reiterated the importance of ensuring home delivery and the continuation of city-specific policy adjustment, alongside the need for a new development model of the housing market and policy measures to absorb inventory stocks and improve new home supply. This may have two implications.

First, housing policy relaxation will likely continue, but a dramatic adjustment from current city-specific to nationwide policy adjustment measures appears unlikely. Further housing policy relaxation may include interest rate cuts (lower mortgage rates), reduction in housing transaction costs (e.g., reduction in stamp duty tax and relaxation in value-added tax for secondary home sales), and further relaxation in home purchase restrictions (HPRs). As of now, the remaining key regions which still have not fully lifted HPRs are: all four tier-1 cities (Guangzhou relaxed the most, with HPR only for small units in core areas), Hainan Province, Tianjin, Hangzhou, Xi<sup>’</sup>an and Zhuhai. A complete removal of HPR would be a positive surprise, but even in that case, it may only lead to a temporary recovery in home transactions. We maintain our cautious view on the commodity housing market, as the market correction is not only cyclical but also structural.

Second, the solution to the housing market (or the new development model) lies in a structural transformation. Equilibrium demand in the commodity housing market may have trended downwards in recent years, and will continue to trend downward due to slowing urbanization, the lower birth rate, lower marriages, population ageing and weaker demand for housing investment. But the potential demand for public housing (including rental housing) is huge. The discrepancy between China<sup>’</sup>s urbanization ratio by citizenship (i.e. hukou) and by residence is about 250 million, or 18% of total population. The new urbanization process (or hukou reform) will create huge potential demand for home replacement i.e. from rural area homes to urban area apartments for millions of beneficiary families.

However, most of the new urbanization process will not generate demand in the commodity housing market, as most of these households are low and middle-income families and not able to afford commodity housing. Hence, public housing (incl. rental housing) will be the solution. This is not new. As shown in Figure 7, public housing has played a more important role in China<sup>’</sup>s urban housing supply since 2010. Prior to 2010 (between 1997 and 2010), new commodity housing accounted for 45% of new urban home supply, while public housing only accounted for 4% but other channels (e.g., employer-subsided housing or when an area is re-classified from rural to urban) accounted for \~50% of new urban housing. In 2010-2022, the share of commodity housing in total new urban housing supply rose to 64%, and the share of public housing rose to 33% (incl. 36 million units of public housing during the 12<sup>th</sup> Five year Plan in 2011-15, 18 million units of shanty-town renovations in 2015-17 and an additional 15 million in 2018-20). However, the pace of public housing increase has slowed since 2020.

Figure 7: Stock of China's urban housing Billion square meters
![](images/f20538f5d689288d1f84b726407c06e7a4b227f5182eabdea352669edaf920a2.jpg)
Source: NBS, CEIC, J.P. Morgan.
Note: "Other housing" includes historical stock (prior to 1995), employer-subsidized housing, new housing supply when an area changes from rural to urban, etc.

In a 2022 note, we pointed out that China<sup>’</sup>s housing market will observe a shift from housing.com to housing.org, or a trend of de-marketization with three features. First, the housing market used to be dominated by commodity housing; going forward we think it will evolve into a multi-pillar structure where the share of public and rental housing will increase. Second, within the commodity housing market, the share of SOE developers will increase at the cost of private developers, in our view. Third, home prices will not be fully market-determined. We believe our views have been verified by the housing market development in the past two years.

In our view, growth in China<sup>’</sup>s public and rental housing market can address the housing market problems. In particular, if public housing or rental housing is supported by the acquisition of unfinished projects or existing inventory in the secondary home market, it can serve multiple policy objectives.

First, the increase in public housing investment can partly offset the contraction in commodity housing investment. An important assumption in our macro forecast this year is a 1 trillion yuan package funded by the central bank (via PSL) to support public housing, urban village renovation and other public infrastructure, which will reduce the decline in real estate investment from 8-10% (without this package) to 2-4% (with this package).

Second, it can accelerate the inventory reduction if public housing is created by the government purchase of unfinished projects in the commodity housing market. Earlier this year, the PBOC approved a 100 billion yuan special facility to support the purchase of commodity housing and its conversion into long-term rental housing in eight trial cities.

Since mid-2023, more than 30 cities have called for “trade in old to buy new<sup>”</sup> in the housing market, which in practice means cities have adopted different approaches: (1) trade-in transaction to sell old secondary housing in core city areas in exchange for new commodity housing, e.g., in Nanjing (Jiangsu); (2) government<sup>’</sup>s subsidies (cash subsidies or rebate of deed tax) to homebuyers who manage to sell their existing residence within a year, e.g., in Suzhou (Jiangsu); (3) SOE acquisition of secondary units and conversion into public or rental housing, e.g., in Zhengzhou (Henan); and (4) allowing homebuyers to cancel a transaction without penalty if their existing residence cannot be sold, e.g., in Shenzhen.

Third, the development in public housing is an indirect form of government transfer to the household sector, an effective way to encourage consumption by beneficiary households and boost domestic demand, so as to mitigate the demandsupply imbalance and deflation pressure in the economy.

## What measures are needed?

The key to the development of public or rental housing, including the above-mentioned “sell old, buy new<sup>”</sup> strategy, is the magnitude of the program and the source of funding. If the magnitude exceeds our assumption of a 1-trillion yuan package, or it evolves into a multi-year project, it could lead to earlier bottoming and recovery in the housing market.

In the near term, given the fiscal difficulties faced by local governments (weak land sales and tightening in LGFV borrowing), the funding may need to come from the central government (e.g., via ultra-long central government bonds) or the central bank (e.g., utilization of PSL, a special fund for home delivery or a special facility to support long-term rental housing). Importantly, if the policymakers can better utilize these resources, or further expand these facilities, it should help offset the weakness in the commodity housing market. Along this line, there is also a policy proposal to establish a specific real estate stabilization fund or a specific institute, to institutionalize the policy support for the housing market.

In the longer run, the source of funding can be rural land reform, involving the transferability of rural homes. The implicit rental increase from such reform may offer a stable funding source for public housing, such as the land-ticket experiment in Chongqing a decade ago that allowed farmers to trade in their rural homes for a land ticket, and the local gov<sup>’</sup>t used the revenue from rural land sales (converting it into higher value usage in industrial or residential land) to provide public housing. Another interesting case, very recently in Lishui City (Zhejiang), the local gov<sup>’</sup>t allowed individuals to bid for residential land (rather than it being restricted to developers). Possible innovations in the urban and rural land systems are important to watch in order to assess the future of China<sup>’</sup>s housing market. While this is not in our baseline assumption, the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum of the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress to be held in July may deliver positive surprises in land reforms.

## Korea: A reality check on private consumption

• 1Q24 private consumption growth was driven by service growth, offsetting drags from goods demand

• Service growth has been boosted by online activities, not limited to reopening demand

BoK’s next growth forecast revision may imply a delayed easing cycle

Real GDP growth surprised to the upside in 1Q24, mainly on domestic demand strength driven by private consumption and construction. The 1Q outcome was at odds with the consensus and the Bank of Korea<sup>’</sup>s view that domestic demand is soft and only gradually recovering. If the domestic demand growth impulse is materially firmer than the BoK<sup>’</sup>s Februrary forecast then monetary policy normalization may be delayed. Here we focus on the private consumption part of the story.

## Consumption growth driven by services

Real private consumption rose 3.2%q/q, saar in 1Q24 despite weak monthly retail sales growth through to March (-0.9%3m/3m, saar). The upside surprise reflects a divergence between quarterly GDP and monthly trackers (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Private consumption in GDP vs. retail sales
![](images/8f71f09483efc7745a12118bc892398d19c23550d1a2b6ce657f93605a86b594.jpg)

The gap between the retail sales and private consumption GDP has widened since the COVID outbreak: consumption growth has been more volatile in the post-COVID period and materially overshot retail sales growth particularly in 2Q21 and 2Q22 (Figure 1). In our view, this divergence (or outperforming of consumption GDP vs. retail sales) likely reflects higher volatility in service activities through the post-COVID period in contrast to before the pandemic.

Separating the contribution to domestic private consumption growth in GDP data by goods and services, service consumption demand clearly outperformed the sluggish goods demand after 2H21 (Figure 2). The initial pent-up demand for goods in 2Q20 and in 1H21 had been the major driver for consumption growth when mobility had not fully recovered. The growth driver shifted to services from 2H21 with the mobility recovery and monetary policy tightening impacting goods demand. While we don<sup>’</sup>t have 1Q24 GDP<sup>’</sup>s details on goods vs. services demand from the flash data, we expect that the services demand should have been firmer than goods demand in 1Q24 GDP based on the monthly data.

Figure 2: Contribution to domestic private consumption growth %pt to %q/q, sa growth
![](images/bc3bd87fa850df14b5a5a61769b2a2fc5786018c4ec9c2652b14b61003f98b7b.jpg)
Source: BoK, and J.P. Morgan

## More than reopening dynamics

What is driving post-COVID service demand growth and is it sustainable? One may argue the recent strength is not sustainable if the demand recovery is narrowly based on reopening dynamics with larger mobility boosting hospitality and leisure services, etc.

Figure 3: Service GDP details
![](images/c31e665c23996694cd7aa136cf9f7e3d48a4d2393537c999f9723f10e60fabf2.jpg)

The detail of the service sector GDP (in the production account) shows that service sector growth has not been narrowly based on reopening demand. In the over-year-ago growth, the distribution (retail and wholesale trade) and hospitality (eating-out and accommodation) services<sup>’</sup> growth accelerated strongly in mid-2022 benefiting from the reopening demand. Yet growth in such in-person service sectors had moderated sharply by mid-2023 as the initial reopening service demand subsided (Figure 3). Meanwhile, services likely related to online activity have been growing strongly through the post-COVID period, including transportation, information and communication services with the online platform services. In all, the reopening benefit appears not to be the sole

driver of the service sector<sup>’</sup>s growth, and the post-COVID structural change toward online activity may also be a longerterm growth factor.

Another potential temporary cause of service sector growth could be cross-border reopening. According to the Korea Tourism Organization<sup>’</sup>s data, Korea<sup>’</sup>s incoming foreign tourist flows are recovering sharply, rising 113.9%oya in 1Q24. Yet at the same time, Korean residents<sup>’</sup> outbound tourism is also rising strongly (+49.1%oya in 1Q with a net outflow), represented by a deepening in the tourism service balance in the BoP data. In the consumption GDP data, such a net outflow of tourism is represented by a rise in net overseas expenditure<sup>’</sup>s contribution (Figure 4). However, the main driver for private consumption has still been expenditure on domestic goods and services, and net overseas expenditure growth should be offset by a fall in net exports limiting the impact on overall GDP growth.

Figure 4: Contribution to private consumption growth by origin %pt to %q/q, sa growth
![](images/9cb4c35d13889bdb3e26fbc3d8c1cee694c75aa72ac2e1908b4235df5068f0cb.jpg)
Source: BoK, and J.P. Morgan
\* Residents' overseas expenditure minus non-residents' expenditure in Korea

## Not too hot, not too cold

Reflecting the upside surprise in 1Q24<sup>’</sup>s real GDP growth, we have upgraded 2024<sup>’</sup>s annual GDP growth outlook from 2.3%y/y to 2.8%. The 2.8% annual growth, if realized, would imply a positive output gap in 2024, returning to around the level seen in 2022 on our estimates. This may suggest a continued hawkish bias to the BoK<sup>’</sup>s monetary policy. Relatively dovish MPC members have cited <sup>‘</sup>sluggish domestic demand<sup>’</sup> as a reason to consider policy rate normalization. Therefore a positive output gap accompanied by resilient private consumption would make an easier policy stance less urgent, or could even lead to further rate hike(s).

Our overall assessment of the growth environment and domestic demand impulse is that we are in a goldilocks zone where the policy stance does not need to shift urgently. 2024<sup>’</sup>s real GDP growth should still be driven mainly by net exports in our view, as our revised growth outlook implies about a 0.8%pt contribution from domestic demand and a 2.0%pt contribution from net exports (Figure 5). Domestic demand<sup>’</sup>s contribution is expected to stay on a downward trend in 2024 after peaking in 2021, mainly reflecting the continued headwinds from the tighter monetary policy stance.

Figure 5: Contribution to real GDP growth
![](images/c50eafd4252d7066ef9ab3a7a66bdd6d70e5218483e20a3f9cc11477100322ac.jpg)

Our current view on the domestic demand impulse (easing gradually in 2024) is based on the balance between the strong 1Q performance and our reading on the details as in this note; and the continued headwinds from the tight policy stance. The headwinds should include 1) elevated debt servicing burden by households, 2) moderation of wage and employment growth in recent quarters, and 3) an expected contraction in construction activities through 2024 with a delayed impact from policy rate hikes. The reading on the strong 1Q growth implies little chance of consumption turning into a recession in 2024 given the structural gain in the service activities trend offsetting the sluggish goods consumption demand.

While domestic demand growth should be firmer than expected before the 1Q GDP data, the contribution from domestic demand is still expected to be lower in 2024 than in the past three years. This feature limits the possibility of the BoK pivoting its policy stance in a dovish direction in the very near term, and at the same time it limits the possibility for the BoK to turn surprisingly hawkish implying a rate hiking policy. The governor mentioned that the BoK<sup>’</sup>s May forecast would be important for revising its forward guidance, and it now looks like the BoK would need to raise the growth outlook materially (currently at 2.1%y/y for 2024). This forecast revision is likely to make the dovish members postpone dissenting votes for a cut, and we see a risk of a prolonged wait-andsee attitude by the BoK unless the inflation trend surprises to the downside.

## United States

• April job growth solid (175k) but below expectations

• Average hourly earnings trend has cooled, but ECI surprised higher

The FOMC stayed on hold as was expected, signaling eventual rate cuts are still the baseline

• Watch Fedspeak next week with limited data releases

The FOMC left rates on hold at its meeting this week and indicated that the economic news from the past few months showed a lack of further progress on inflation. The Committee clearly doesn<sup>’</sup>t have the confidence on inflation it needs to start lowering rates in the very near term. But the FOMC still believes that rates currently are restrictive and that the desired progress on inflation will come at some point, allowing it to lower interest rates.

Many economic indicators have surprised to the upside in recent months, pushing back expectations for the first Fed cut. But the April employment report (released two days after the end of the May FOMC meeting) disappointed in many respects: job growth, wage growth, and the workweek all surprised to the downside, while the unemployment rate unexpectedly moved up. To be sure, the April data were not weak, and on its own the latest jobs report probably is not enough to cause policymakers to meaningfully change their expected path for policy. But if the softening evident in April is followed by additional cooling in future reports, we think this could prompt the Fed to move on rates. We continue to look for the first cut to come at the July FOMC meeting.

The other reports from the past week were somewhat mixed. The ECI showed an upside surprise with respect to labor costs in 1Q, but the JOLTS report suggested that supply and demand in the labor market have been approaching better balance lately. Jobless claims data point to strength in the labor market continuing through most of April, whereas a variety of April survey data (i.e., the ISM reports and the Conference Board<sup>’</sup>s consumer survey) disappointed. There wasn<sup>’</sup>t a lot of news related to GDP, and we continue to forecast 2.5% real GDP growth for 2Q. Next week<sup>’</sup>s scheduled data flow is pretty quiet, but we should start to see some of the reaction to the April jobs report from various Fed officials that are scheduled to speak throughout the week.

## Labor market: Goldilocks is back

As noted above, the April jobs report was a rare downside surprise among a series of stronger-than-expected readings in the recent past. Employment growth eased in April, in addition to softer readings on the average workweek and earnings growth. This welcome news followed a JOLTS report earlier in the week that also showed further signs of cooling labor demand and hiring but no signs of rising separations. Low and steady weekly jobless claims continued to signal that this gradual rebalancing of the labor market is happening without a significant uptick in job loss. One unwelcome surprise this week was the firmer-than-expected ECI print for 1Q.

Nonfarm payroll growth slowed to 175,000 last month, below the 269,000 average gain over the prior three months and the lowest reading since October (Figure 1). The public sector was a big contributor to the step down in job growth reported for April, with only 8,000 government jobs added that month—down from the 60,000 average pace in the prior six months. Private service sector employment grew at a solid clip in April (153,000), with health care and social assistance accounting for over half of these jobs (87,000). In another sign of moderating labor demand, the average workweek ticked down to 34.3 hours (Figure 2). The average workweek for production and non-supervisory workers also ticked down, to 33.7 hours.

Figure 1: Nonfarm payroll employment 000s, sa monthly change
![](images/52f455c781d091c1410c36172e11afe4941d8fd36bba85b48085cc1ba797923b.jpg)

Figure 2: Average weekly hours
![](images/450d345ace0fbf76fdac27f92b0be6b9dccf2a017f664e52cdaefbbb49d8e108.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

In the household survey, the key variables exhibited only moderate changes. The unemployment rate ticked up from 3.8% to 3.9% in April, after rounding. The changes in household employment (25,000) and unemployment (63,000) were relatively modest in April. Even though this gain was much higher (563,000) when adjusted for the payroll concept of employment, the household survey continued to show weaker job growth than the establishment survey on net in recent months. Finally, the participation rate held at 62.7% in April and the employment-to-population ratio declined 0.1%-pt to 60.2% after rising by 0.2%-pt in March.

On the wage front, the April jobs report also delivered a positive surprise for those hoping for softer inflation. Average hourly earnings increased only 0.2% in April and, with downward revisions, the year-ago figure cooled to 3.9%. On a 3-month and 12-month annualized basis, the latest readings on average hourly earnings (2.8% and 3.9%, respectively) were the softest seen in some time. Relative to this positive news, earlier in the week we saw a firmer-than-expected ECI reading for 1Q. Year-ago growth in ECI and average hourly earnings were relatively close by the end of 1Q (Figure 3), but we see reasons that ECI growth might stay a bit firmer for the rest of the year. Since the ECI is seen as a better gauge of wage inflation, policymakers will be looking for further evidence of cooling in wage inflation beyond one month<sup>’</sup>s average hourly earnings. The more volatile unit labor cost data also accelerated in 1Q, rising 4.7%q/q, saar, although the year-ago pace eased to 1.8%oya.

Figure 4: ISM survey headlines
Figure 3: Select measures of employment costs %oya, end of quarter
![](images/90d20459cb379e68a589f7675cca89971d10134dd07b105d15ef564e6d356654.jpg)

Finally, job openings continued to decline in March according to the JOLTS report this week, which also showed declines in hires and quits during that month. These all point to some softening in labor market conditions with demand and supply likely coming into a better balance into the second quarter.

## Powell still sees eventual rate cuts

The modest cooling in the April employment report was arguably supportive of the tone from the May FOMC meeting, as Chair Powell kept the door open for rate cuts later this year. He acknowledged that the recent run of data going into this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting “have not given us that greater confidence<sup>”</sup> that inflation is coming down, and thus gaining that confidence is likely to “take longer than previously expected.<sup>”</sup> That said, he also steered clear of any suggestion for now that the next move could be a hike. Rather, any further persistence in inflation would lead the Fed to “hold off on rate cuts.<sup>”</sup>

In addition to inflation coming back down, Powell also noted that “an unexpected weakening in the labor market<sup>”</sup> could trigger cuts, but that would have to be more than a few tenths higher on the unemployment rate, and, of course, the Fed won<sup>’</sup>t react to just one softer jobs report. Instead, the latest jobs report likely furthers the progress toward “better balance<sup>”</sup> in the labor market that various Fed officials have sought. While there remains a path for rate cuts to start in July as we expect, risks are still skewed toward a later start. Markets are now pricing nearly a full rate cut by September. Unsurprisingly, Powell was adamant that the election would not preclude the Fed from moving at its September meeting if the data support it.

More substantive changes were made to QT, as the cap on the monthly runoff of Treasury holdings was reduced to \$25bn from \$60bn previously, effective June 1. While a slightly larger reduction than expected (the March minutes had suggested cutting the pace in half), this decision had been flagged at the March meeting and thus was not a big surprise.

## Business surveys stumble in Apri

The softer momentum signaled by the April employment report was amplified by weaker business surveys last month. The headline ISM indexes for both the manufacturing and services surveys disappointed in April, respectively down 1.1 and 2.0 points from their March readings (Figure 4). But these two surveys are seeing notably different trends of late: despite the April setback, the manufacturing ISM has been improving on net, whereas the services ISM plunged to its second-lowest level this cycle—and a relatively weak level historically of 49.2. The April declines were broad-based across the ISM components, with the biggest drops in the manufacturing production and services business activity indexes. While this loss of momentum bears watching, it<sup>’</sup>s important to recognize that the ISMs do not map closely into key economic activity measures.

We forecast that initial claims increased 12,000 to 220,000 during the week ending May 4. Claims have been pretty steady at low levels lately, suggesting strength in the labor market. The seasonal factors anticipate filings to move down during the week ending May 4 before seasonal adjustment. But we think that filings may not decline by as much as anticipated by the seasonal factors given that spring breaks have occurred lately in various areas and these might generate a temporary increase in filings, and we believe this will result in an upcoming increase in seasonally adjusted filings.

Index (nsa)</td><td>76.9</td><td>79.4</td><td>77.2</td><td>77.5</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Current conditions</td><td>79.4</td><td>82.5</td><td>79.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Expectations</td><td>75.2</td><td>77.4</td><td>76.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Short-term</td><td>3.0</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Long term</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

We look for consumer sentiment to inch up 0.3pt to 77.5 in the preliminary May survey from the University of Michigan. This measure has been trending up in a choppy manner from its post-pandemic lows in mid-2022, although it has been more narrowly rangebound over the past five months. We see continued labor market strength, including a steady and low level of jobless claims, as modestly supportive for sentiment in May. Other news has been more mixed: retail gasoline prices are up nearly \$0.60/gal since the start of the year but have stabilized in recent weeks; stock prices have recovered a little less than half of their nearly 6% decline from late March through mid-April; mortgage rates have eased slightly in recent weeks but are still more than 50bp above their recent dip below 7% in early February. Surveyed inflation expectations also will be closely watched after rising slightly last month, given recent firmness in consumer inflation readings.

The employment cost index (ECI) increased 1.2% in 1Q, which was an upside surprise relative to expectations and the firmest quarterly print since 3Q22 (although with rounding, the 1Q23 change also came in at 1.2%). Looking past some of the quarterly noise in the data, the ECI was reported up 4.2% oya in 1Q. This year-ago print was unchanged from the 4Q23 figure (with rounding), down noticeably from the recent high (5.1% oya in 2Q22), but still up meaningfully from the norms from pre-pandemic years (the local pre-pandemic peak was 2.9% oya in 4Q18). Overall, the ECI suggests—like many labor market indicators—that the labor market has remained tight lately, even though the imbalance between labor demand and labor supply has been reduced in recent years.

Consumer confidence (Apr 30)

The Conference Board<sup>’</sup>s consumer confidence index disappointed in April, with the headline dropping from 103.1 in March to 97.0. The index has now declined in three straight months, and this latest reading was the lowest since July 2022 and one of the weakest since since early 2021. The present situation subcomponent and the expectations subcomponent both have moved down in recent months, but of the two the latest print on the expectations measure looks relatively more downbeat. The Conference Board survey also showed a large step-down in its labor market differential in April, with this measure falling from 29.5 to 25.3. While the labor market differential has declined over the past few months and has moved down notably off of the highs from a few years ago, the latest figure is still solid relative to the overall history of the series. The separate gauge of consumer attitudes reported by the University of Michigan also has cooled a bit lately, but the recent trend for this measure looks more upbeat than what we see in the Conference Board survey. And the “hard data<sup>”</sup> on consumer spending also has performed pretty well lately (granted these figures are more lagging than the sentiment surveys).

The April ISM manufacturing survey disappointed, with the headline moving down from 50.3 in March to 49.2. This April decline reversed part of the 2.5pt increase reported for March, and the headline index still has improved on net lately despite the softening reported for this latest month. Similarly, the headline for the manufacturing PMI survey has moved higher on net despite cooling a bit in April. Within the ISM survey, key details on production and new orders softened in April, although the declines that month (3.3pts and 2.3pts, respectively) came after improvement reported for March. The survey<sup>’</sup>s employment index moved up a bit in April but stayed soft, rising from 47.4 to 48.6. The survey's prices index continued its recent move higher, with a 5.1pt increase to 60.9 reported for April. Some of the recent gains for prices may be seasonal in nature (this prices series is not seasonally adjusted), but even over the past year, this index has climbed

7.7pts.

Construction spending (May 1)

Total construction spending declined 0.2% in March, an outcome just below our forecast but more of a disappointment relative to the consensus (revisions were mixed for recent earlier months). On net, the data from the construction report had basically no noticeable effect on headline GDP tracking for 1Q. In terms of some of the details, private construction spending declined 0.5% in March, with declines on both the residential and nonresidential side. Public spending, meanwhile, rose 0.8% in March, with this gain coming after backto-back monthly declines. While performance has been mixed across categories and months lately, the trends for all three main subcategories of the construction report have softened and the headline aggregate has started to move lower recently after a run of monthly gains. The headline construction series declined 3.2% saar over the most recent three months after jumping almost 15% over the prior twelve.

<table><tr><td>Thousands, sa</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td></tr><tr><td>Job openings</td><td>8748</td><td>8756</td><td>8813</td><td>8520 8488</td></tr><tr><td>Hires</td><td>5698</td><td>5818</td><td>5781</td><td>5500</td></tr><tr><td>Separations</td><td>5449</td><td>5559</td><td>5539</td><td>5200</td></tr><tr><td>Quits</td><td>3446</td><td>3484</td><td>3527</td><td>3329</td></tr><tr><td>Layoffs</td><td>1596</td><td>1724</td><td>1681</td><td>1526</td></tr></table>

The March JOLTS report showed declines in job openings, hires, and quits during the month, all of which suggest some softening in labor market conditions relative to February. In more favorable news, however, the number of layoffs also declined in March. On the whole, we think the JOLTS data suggest that conditions are moderating in the labor market and gross hiring has slowed, but there still appears to be relatively strong demand for labor. In terms of some of the main numbers, the number of job openings declined from 8.813mn to 8.488mn in March, coming out a little below our forecast and showing more of a downside surprise relative to the consensus. This latest move lower continued what has been a downward trend for openings lately and the March figure was the lowest level seen in about three years. But openings remain up significantly relative to pre-pandemic norms. Similarly, the vacancy-to-unemployment ratio also has been moving down lately, but remains high by pre-pandemic standards—this inched down from 1.4 in February to 1.3 in March after rounding.

With quits moving down to 3.329mn in March (and the quits rate declining to 2.1%), the latest data are softer than the figures seen in the period immediately preceding the spread of COVID. But even so, the latest figures still signal a relatively high willingness to quit jobs by broad historical standards, which is often associated with strong labor markets. Another sign of labor market strength in the JOLTS report is the layoffs series, which has been bouncing around at very low levels in recent years.

The nominal trade deficit was little changed in March, moving from -\$69.5bn in February to -\$69.4bn. Exports and imports both fell in March, moving down 2.0% and 1.6%, respectively. Even with trade pulling back in March, there were strong gains overall for both imports and exports in 1Q (particularly imports). The recent 1Q GDP report showed trade and inventories weighing meaningfully on GDP growth in 1Q and a modest increase in real equipment investment during the quarter, and with the figures currently in hand we expect the next GDP report from the BEA to show similar stories for these different groupings.

The preliminary release for 1Q nonfarm labor productivity edged up 0.3% q/q saar, which was a notable slowdown from recent prior quarters and a slight disappointment to consensus expectations, albeit above our forecast. These data are volatile, however, and even with the soft quarterly reading, yearago productivity rose to 2.9%oya from 2.7% in 4Q23. Output growth was notably softer last quarter—as seen in the last

GDP report—while hours grew solidly. Labor productivity had been running at a particularly strong pace for much of last year (with 4Q23 revised 0.2%-pts higher to 3.5%ar in this report) and some reversion lower was likely, but we suspect the underlying trend for productivity growth remains stronger than this preliminary quarterly growth suggests.

Also in the report, unit labor costs surged 4.7% q/q saar in 1Q—although the year-ago pace eased to 1.8%oya. Again, these data are noisy, with last quarter<sup>’</sup>s year-ago reading at the low end of the range since mid-2021. That said, we take a stronger signal of cooling but still elevated wage growth from the ECI.

(%)</td><td>3.9</td><td></td><td>3.8</td><td></td><td>38</td><td>3.9</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%)</td><td>62.5</td><td></td><td>62.7</td><td></td><td></td><td>62.7</td></tr></table>

All the important figures in the April jobs report came in a little softer than expected, and a little softer than the recent trend. Nonfarm employment growth eased to 175,000 last month, below the 269,000 average gain over the prior three months. In another sign of moderating labor demand, the average workweek ticked down to 34.3 hours. The unemployment rate rounded up from 3.8% in March to 3.9% last month. Finally, average hourly earnings increased only 0.2% in April, and with downward revisions, the year-ago figure cooled off to 3.9%. While all these figures were softer than prior months, they were still generally solid. Nominal labor income growth has been running around 5% in recent months, which should continue to provide the fuel for steady consumer spending growth. Overall, we<sup>’</sup>re still comfortable projecting that real GDP growth will pick up to about a 2.5% annualized pace this quarter. Fed officials will likely view this repor as a welcome sign that the labor market is continuing to come into better balance and that policy is restrictive enough to cool off labor demand (particularly with large immigration flows raising the break-even level of monthly employment growth to 200,000 or higher). We<sup>’</sup>re sticking with our call for a first ease in July. The market is not there but we believe that if the next two job reports show continued cooling in labor market activity, then the FOMC will be comfortable taking

back some of its policy restraint.

The 175,000 jobs added in the establishment survey in April marked the softest monthly gain since October. Prior months were revised down 22,000 on net. The public sector was a big contributor to the step down in job growth reported for April, as only 8,000 government jobs were added that month, down from the 60,000 average pace from the prior six months. The private service sector continued to add jobs at a solid clip in April (153,000), with health care and social assistance accounting for over half of these jobs (87,000). The move down in the average workweek also occurred in the workweek for production and non-supervisory workers, which ticked down to 33.7 hours. On a 3-month and 12-month annualized basis, the latest readings on average hourly earnings (2.8% and 3.9%, respectively) were the softest seen in some time. This moderation in wage growth has been reasonably broad-based across industries. The production worker earnings series similarly has shown moderation in wage growth lately with the 3-month annualized gain in this series only 2.6%.

In the household survey, the key variables exhibited only moderate changes. The unemployment rate ticked up from 3.8% to 3.9% in April, although at a higher level of precision the move up was just 4bps. And the changes in household employment (25,000) and unemployment (63,000) were relatively modest in April. The participation rate held at 62.7% in April and the employment-to-population ratio declined 0.1%pt to 60.2% that month after rising by 0.2%pt in March. The household survey did show strong employment growth in April (563,000) when adjusting to be closer to the establishment survey<sup>’</sup>s payroll concept of employment. But even with this strong April gain, the adjusted series in the household survey still has shown weaker job growth than the establishment survey on net in recent months. The U-6 broad measure of labor market underutilization ticked up to 7.4% and is now 0.9%-point above its late <sup>’</sup>22 low of 6.5%.

The final release of the April services PMI showed a more modest decline from March, down just 0.4-pts to 51.3—and upwardly revised from the flash reading of 50.9 last month. In historical context, this level is roughly aligned with its prepandemic average. Note that the large decline in the services PMI employment index was only slightly attenuated from the flash to final release: it now stands at 47.7, a 3.4-pt drop (rather than a 3.8-pt decline previously). The loss of momentum signaled by both the ISM and PMI employment components was greater than the modest slowing in private services payroll employment in the April jobs report.

## ISM services survey (May 3)

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Sa</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Headline composite</td><td>Feb 52.6</td><td>51.4</td><td>51.0 49.4</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>57.2</td><td>57.4</td><td>50.9</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>56.1</td><td>54.4</td><td>52.2</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>48.0</td><td>48.5</td><td>45.9</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td>58.6</td><td>53.4</td><td>59.2</td></tr></table>

The April services ISM disappointed, with the headline composite measure declining 2.0 points to 49.4—marking the lowest reading since the onset of the pandemic outside of a one-off spike lower (to 49.0) in December 2022, and a relatively weak reading historically. The underlying components were generally softer across the board, with business activity posting an outsized 6.5-point drop to 50.7, while new orders fell 2.2-pts and employment dropped 2.6-pts. Meanwhile, the prices component jumped 5.8-pts to 59.2, more than reversing the prior month<sup>’</sup>s decline and putting the level roughly back to where it was a year ago. That said, it has been volatile in recent months. Overall, this was a downbeat report, and, combined with the 1.1-pt drop in the manufacturing ISM earlier this week, the composite index dropped to 49.4 from 51.3 in March, also near a cycle low—although the links between this survey and key economic statistics are not particularly tight.

## Focus: Firm details of ECI headline have some room to run

In a week full of labor market data, the ECI release delivered a modest upside surprise in 1Q, rising 1.2% during the quarter and moving up 4.2%oya. Many analysts focused on the strength in two different segments within the ECI, as there was outperformance relative to the headline for both state and local government workers (1.3%q/q) and union workers (1.7%q/q, unadjusted). If history is any guide, compensation growth in these two segments may continue to be firm as there remains room for catchup relative to earlier growth in other sectors. That said, these two segments (which likely overlap to some degree) constitute a relatively small share of the workforce, hence continued firmness in their ECIs might have a limited effect on the headline.

Figure 1: ECI compensation growth - private vs government %Ch sa, 4q
![](images/d0b8f73fbfa64f080a571aa3919d439bf4029c94f4fb127f6b3367a91e6f2be7.jpg)

Figure 2: ECI compensation for private vs government
![](images/5cc1a0d2e679c574dad63adeafdf2ecef8f440d71f21ff9523218bc59e56d943.jpg)

Although the private sector compensation index firmed up in 1Q, the oya figure continued to moderate from 4% in 4Q to 3.9% in 1Q (Figure 1). The quarterly growth in this component (1.1%) was essentially the same as the average reading over the prior three years. In contrast, the related index for state and local government workers increased by 1.4% in 1Q, significantly above its average quarterly rate of 1% over the prior three years, bringing the oya figure to 4.8%. Despite this relative firmness in the government sector, because its share

is below 13% of nonfarm employment, it did not cause a significant firming in the headline figure and kept it flat on a year-ago basis (Figure 1). ECI for the government sector tends to grow at the same rate as the private sector in the long run and periods of divergence between them are followed by a process of catching up (Figure 2). We think this is a possibility in the near term, as this sector catches up with market compensation and comes in a bit firmer than the private sector.

A similar process might be playing out for the compensation of union workers. These two stories also might be related, since public workers have higher union membership (32%) than the overall workforce (10%). After a period of underperformance relative to nonunion workers, ECI compensation for union workers surged in 1Q, bringing the oya figure to 5.3%, the highest in almost two decades (Figure 3). High-profile labor actions last year led to new long-term contracts with significant wage gains and these likely played a role in the recent rise in this measure. Due to the long-term nature of these contracts, compensation growth for union workers relative to the rest historically lagged the tightness in the labor market (Figure 4). As such, we think there might still be some room for firm readings for union workers over the next few quarters. However, the size of the unionized workforce is about 6% of the private sector, limiting the potential impact on the overall ECI index, but likely partially offsetting the impact of the gradual softening in labor market conditions.

Figure 3: ECI compensation growth by worker bargaining status %Ch nsa, 4q
![](images/98993c5cb83bcd9236d22cd2a6692c87debc57ed848f7e4c41757116823f2b5c.jpg)

## Euro Area

• 1Q GDP surprised to the upside at 1.3% ar

• Core inflation declined further, but less than expected

• Unemployment rate remained stable at a historical low

This week saw the Euro area deliver upside surprises on growth and core inflation. The 1.3%q/q saar gain in GDP was more than double our forecast and with less of this due to mild weather than expected. National data also hint at a pickup in consumption. Separately, core inflation declined less than expected, with services prices looking sticky over the Easter period at around 0.4%m/m, sa. Core goods brought some partial relief, however, with another fall in prices. At the same time, GDP deflator inflation may have slowed by 1.5%-pt, judging by the available national data, which would reflect weaker price pressures coming from unit profits. As wage growth is likely to slow with a delay and productivity still weak, this is an important offset to the current strength in unit labour costs. Near-term inflation risks remain on the upside, but the data are not showing one-way traffic.

While we still think that Euro area inflation can slow to 2% without a weakening in the labour market, reflecting the region<sup>’</sup>s low inflation past and that the past inflation surge is still a driver of wages, it may take longer for this to show clearly in the data, especially against an improving growth backdrop. As a result, we have slowed our expectations for ECB cuts to 75bp year year and also see modestly slower cuts next year. The end-point for inflation and rates remains at 2%, however, with risks still skewed higher on both.

## Stronger-than-expected GDP gain

According to the Eurostat flash release, the Euro area economy grew 1.3%q/q saar in 1Q24, in contrast with broadly flat readings seen during 2H23. The 1Q gain was stronger than we and consensus expected (0.5%ar), and stronger than the signal of the PMI, which pointed to a 0.5% ar gain (Figure 1).

![](images/84b2554426ff132782f5eabf25d266dd0e073a0fdd8a3924e4b80428e7c6e1a8.jpg)

No details were provided with the Euro area flash estimate but country-level information hinted at a decent contribution from domestic final sales (France and Spain) and household spending. This development echoes our view that real income gains should boost household consumption. Country level data so far also suggested that the 1Q gain was likely driven by services while manufacturing was weak. This manufacturing-services divide also was at play last year. The mild weather also supported construction in Germany last quarter.

On the pricing side, it is likely that the over year ago change in the Euro area GDP deflator slowed based on country level information (in particular Germany) which hints at more modest domestic inflationary pressures. But despite the decline, the data (4.5%oya in Germany, 3.8% in France and 3.2%oya in Spain) are still significantly above the level consistent with the 2% ECB objective. Additional country details will be available in late May with important information about unit labor costs and unit profits. These details will be key to monitor as the ECB expects unit profits to come down this year (some hints in this direction could be seen in the 1Q Spanish GDP report), which would contribute to lower domestic inflation.

Looking at the country details, real GDP was up 0.9% ar in Germany, 0.9% ar in France, 1.2% ar in Italy, 2.9% ar in Spain , 1.2% ar in Belgium and 4.4% ar in Ireland. Upward surprises relative to our forecasts were broad based across countries. Excluding Ireland, the 1Q24 Euro area gain is 1.1%q/q ar.

Figure 2: Euro area real GDP
![](images/1bad277ad17d3abe5542d680f0017d90ea24d1967802df081171af8b1f6158bc.jpg)

The April PMI was stronger than we expected last week and had already prompted us to revise up our Euro area 2Q GDP forecast (from 0.75% ar to 1.0%ar). We are sticking with our forecast for now but acknowledge upside risks. First, the 1Q GDP print came in stronger than suggested by the PMI. Second, the PMI has been on a steep trend up and already hinted at a 1.2% ar activity gain at the start of 2Q. For now, we prefer to wait for the May PMI to reassess our forecast. Other data releases by then will also provide important insights to assess activity in the early part of 2024.

## Core inflation declined less than expected

According to Eurostat flash release, Euro area HICP headline inflation was down 0.05%-pt to 2.38%oya in April, with core inflation down 0.28%-pt to 2.67%oya. Both readings were a bit higher than our 2.3% and 2.6%forecasts, respectively. Looking at the limited details at hand, the decline in core inflation was both driven by core goods, down 0.2%-pt to 0.9%oya and services, down 0.3%-pt to 3.7%oya. We expected services price inflation to come down further, due to some payback from the early Easter (a boost in March and a drag in April), but this effect was likely less strong than we had assumed. Outside the core basket, energy price inflation was up 1.2%-pt to -0.6%oya and food price inflation increased 0.2%-pt to 2.8%oya.

In seasonally adjusted terms, core prices were up 0.15%m/m sa in April, with core goods prices down 0.16%m/m and services prices up 0.32%m/m. Core goods prices have weakened in March and April after modest gains in January and February. The fading of inflationary pressures (supply side, gas, electricity) and weak goods demand are limiting goods prices, but the decline in April was nevertheless a surprise. However, services prices remain sticky. Services monthly price gains have averaged 0.43%m/m since the start of the year. Tax increases and price resetting (insurance, in particular) overall boosted this number a little, while the early Easter effect created some volatility over March/April. But, getting rid of the noise, services prices were probably still running at a strong 4% ar pace.

Source: Eurostat, ECB, and J.P.Morgan

After taking into account this week<sup>’</sup>s upward core surprise, we left our 2Q core inflation forecast at 2.7%oya. That this forecast has not gone up reflects some changes in our assumption about the early Easter effect. We had assumed some upward impact in May that we are now getting rid of.

However, the chances of core inflation settling in line with the 2.5%oya ECB forecast in 2Q are slim, in our view. The composition of inflation also suggests that services prices are sticky, hinting at still firm domestic inflationary pressures. Beyond 2Q, we assume that core inflation will come down to 2.6%oya in 2H24, before moving gradually towards 2.0%oya in 2H25. The risk around our medium-term forecast is tilted to the upside. Reinforcing this is the sense that Euro area GDP growth could outperform our trend-like forecast.

## Unemployment rate stable at 6.5%

While the unemployment rate was stable at its historical low of 6.5% in March, the number of unemployed declined by 94,000 (Figure 4). The unemployment rate has been stable over the past year despite GDP growth averaging only a 0.4% ar. The counterpart to this is weakness in productivity and that looks to have continued in 1Q24 despite the improvement in GDP growth. This is suggested by the PMI employment index and could reflect labor shortages remaining above their historical norms, particularly in the services sector. Those shortages may bias firms to recruiting even if that means accepting a fall in output per worker. A shortening workweek has also biased employment higher. Finally, unemployment rates still differ significantly at the country level. The unemployment rate stands at 3.2% in Germany, while the it stands at 11.7% in Spain. France (7.3%) and Italy (7.2%) are intermediate cases.

![](images/5d399803d03653b7e251a95274259c344c83bcc73e9a4e20f4ff8694e36cf1bb.jpg)

The new orders index in the German manufacturing PMI slipped from 42.5 in March to 40.5 in April, suggesting that orders are still sliding in an underlying sense. Actual orders data have been very noisy recently due to large aircraft orders, but settled down in February. We have pencilled in a modest increase in March, driven by ex-bulk orders, which have been weaker than total orders recently. That would however still leave a weak underlying trend, consistent with the surveys.

Industrial production
<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td></tr><tr><td>Wed</td><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>May 8</td><td>Industry incl constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-2.0</td><td>1.3</td><td>2.1</td><td>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>-3.5</td><td>-5.1</td><td>-4.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Industry ex constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-1.8</td><td>1.0</td><td>1.1</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Manufacturing (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-2.4</td><td>1.4</td><td>1.8</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Construction (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-3.1</td><td>2.9</td><td>7.9</td><td>-5.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Energy (%m/m, sa)</td><td>5.0</td><td>-3.6</td><td>-8.2</td><td>5.0</td></tr></table>

German IP rose solidly in both January and February. The gain in February was likely helped by mild-weather, as suggested by the fall in energy output and the jump in construction output. Some reversal of this is likely in March. But, manufacturing has also performed better with Jan/Feb around 2%ar above the 4Q23 average. This is running ahead of the business surveys as the output index in the German manufacturing PMI has only recovered to 45pt so far. Hence, some softening in March is possible although the VDA car produc tion data were stable in March.

Demand and labor markets
<table><tr><td colspan="2">Retail sales</td><td rowspan="2">Jan</td><td rowspan="2">Feb</td><td rowspan="2">Mar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Dec</td></tr><tr><td>Tue May 7</td><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>%m/m</td><td>-0.6 -0.6</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td></td><td>-0.9</td><td>-0.7</td><td></td></tr></table>

Euro area retail sales overall have moved sideways in recent months. But this outcome hides significant disparities at the country level. Among the main economies of the region, France stands out as retail sales have been on an upward trend since late last year. Meanwhile, retail sales have declined in Italy and Spain over this period. GDP preliminary GDP releases overall suggest that Euro area consumer consumption was decent in 1Q, but the details suggest that this outcome was driven by the services sector.

Real GDP grew a stronger-than-expected 0.9%q/q saar in Germany, although we had flagged upside risk to our cautious estimate of 0.25%q/q saar. The 0.9%-pt downward revision to

4Q23 to -2.0%q/q saar was counteracted by upward revisions to prior quarters. No details are available, except that the statistics office said that higher construction and exports lifted growth. The construction part of this is likely weather-related, but we note that the business surveys are showing an underlying pickup into 2Q24. The only other information is a stall in the German GDP deflator (0%q/q saar), which pulled the annual gain 2%-pt to 4.5%oya. That is still firm, but clearly less firm than before. It is unclear what drove this slowing. We suspect that unit labour costs were still strong, which would suggest some softening from unit profits. But, this is speculation at this stage.

Italian GDP rose 1.2%q/q saar in 1Q24, which was more than the 0.5%q/q saar that were expected. This is a modest pickup from last year<sup>’</sup>s pace of 0.5%ar and a bit ahead of the signal from the PMI for 1Q. No details are available, except for a few comments form the statistics office. These suggested that almost all sectors of the economy expanded (agriculture, industry and services). On the demand side, there appears to have been a negative contribution from domestic demand, whereas net trade was a positive.

<table><tr><td></td><td>3Q23</td><td colspan="2">4Q23</td><td colspan="2">1Q24</td></tr><tr><td>Spain (flash estimate)</td><td></td><td colspan="2"></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, sa</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.7 05</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, saar</td><td>17</td><td>1.9</td><td>23 2.7</td><td>20</td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>1.9</td><td>20</td><td>2.1</td><td></td><td>2.4</td></tr></table>

The Spanish economy continued to grow strongly in 1Q24. Real GDP rose 2.9%q/q saar and there were also modest upward revisions to the two prior quarters. The level of real

GDP is now 3.7% above the pre-pandemic level, which is ahead of the Euro area average. Growth in 1Q24 was driven by domestic finales sales (+1.8%-pt contribution) and net exports (+2.0%-pt contribution), while inventory changes were a drag (0.8%-pt contribution). On the domestic side, consumer spending was up 1.2%q/q saar, government consumption was down 3.8%q/q saar and capex was up 10.9%q/ q saar, reflecting strong gains in construction and machinery/ equipment. The net trade contribution reflected strength in both exports (9.9%q/q saar) and imports (4.6%q/q saar). On the production side, growth was equally broad based across manufacturing, construction and services.

In other details, the GDP deflator was up 2.6%q/q saar and 3.2%oya. This is a slowing from prior quarters that saw the GDP deflator still up around 6%oya. Unit labour costs (5.3%q/q saar and 4.7%oya) are the main factor holding the GDP deflator up, while unit profit growth is broadly stable (0.6%oya). This is the pattern assumed in the ECB staff forecast, although the ECB has some outright declines in unit profits for the Euro area in 1H24, of which there is no sign yet in the Spanish data. The strength in unit labour costs reflects strength in compensation per employee (+5.9%q/q saar and 4.1%oya) and weakness in labour productivity (+0.5%q/q saar and -0.6%oya). Productivity is better on a per hour basis though. The labour market continued to generate strong jobs growth (+2.4%q/q saar and 3%oya), but a shortening workweek has offset half of this over the past year. This explains why productivity is better on a per hour than on a per worker basis. Details on household real incomes and the saving rate are not yet available, but, given the strong growth in wages, real incomes are likely expanding solidly.

Demand and labor markets

<table><tr><td>Retail sales Mar</td></tr><tr><td>Jan Feb</td></tr><tr><td>Germany</td></tr><tr><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa %m/m 0.3 -0.1 47 -1.5</td></tr><tr><td>12 1.8 %oya 1.2 -1.0 25 -2.2 0.3</td></tr><tr><td></td></tr></table>

The 1.8%m/m increase in German retail sales came after a 1.5%m/m fall in February and still leaves 1Q24 down 3.8%q/ q saar. But it sets up a better comparison for 2Q24. It is always hard to know what causes the volatility in German retail sales. The weather is one candidate over February and March, but from a real income perspective, German disposable incomes are only now starting to pick up and therefore past weakness in retail sales may also relate to this. This turn partly reflects the large one-off payments in some recent pay deals that are coming through, including at the end of 1Q24, combined with the slowing in headline inflation.

<table><tr><td>Unemployment</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Harmonized measure, sa (Eurostat) Unemployment rate (%)</td><td>6.5</td><td></td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Unemployment (ch m/m, 000s)</td><td>9.0</td><td>21.0 17.0</td><td>22.0</td><td></td><td>-94.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Registered (ch m/m, 000s, sa)</td><td>11</td><td>12</td><td>5</td><td>6 0</td><td>10</td></tr><tr><td>000s, nsa</td><td>2813.8</td><td>2769.3</td><td></td><td></td><td>2749.6</td></tr><tr><td>Unempl. rate (%, sa)</td><td>5.9</td><td></td><td>5.9</td><td>5.9</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jan</td><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Change m/m, 000s, sa</td><td>53</td><td>1</td><td>16</td><td>17 25</td><td>8</td></tr></table>

The German labour market data were resilient in level terms and continued the recent trends. Unemployment continued to nudge higher in April (+10,000). The unemployment rate held at 5.9% on the national measure and at 3.2% on the harmonized measure. Vacancies continued to fall in terms of those registered with job agencies, but the more comprehensive IAB data remain much higher. Employment rose 8,000 in March, which is modest and suggests that unemployment is nudging higher as the level of employment growth is still falling short of the growth of the labour force. Finally, short-time work usage appears to have moved a bit higher recently, but without showing any dramatic changes. Overall, the labour market is likely to firm modestly in the near-term given the improvement in the latest business surveys for overall growth.
