## Japan

• Yen depreciation met with likely FX intervention response this week

• Capital outflow pressure to persist against backdrop of Japan’s negative real rates

But FX depreciation is also serving to loosen monetary conditions materially

• Watching for spillovers from weak yen into profits, incomes, and spending

• Next week: April wage growth remains resilient

The immediate focus this week was on multiple rounds of (likely) FX intervention that managed to cap USD/JPY<sup>’</sup>s ascent around the optically-important 160 threshold. Despite the prospect of continued intervention, the risk is that the yen continues to take its cue from deeply negative real rates in Japan alongside higher global core yields; this mix points to the potential for sustained capital outflow pressure. Last week<sup>’</sup>s BoJ meeting implied to us that this fundamental backdrop is unlikely to change dramatically, even if we are right in projecting a July hike to 25bp and a more explicit shift in balance sheet policy that potentially comes earlier.

But it would be a mistake, we think, to interpret the yen<sup>’</sup>s slide as an entirely unintended (or unexpected) side-effect of monetary policy. Of course, rapid, non-linear currency depreciation risks catalyzing a sharp shift in household portfolio allocations away from yen deposits; this is not the baseline outlook. On the flip side, the fall in the yen<sup>’</sup>s real effective exchange rate (down around 8% this year, and stuck at multidecade lows) is also contributing to a significant loosening in domestic monetary conditions.

1982=0, cumulative monthly chg. in JPY REER and real BoJ policy rate, equal wtd.

![](images/302e4a83aac04164b5f21661790a6953cdfe366e8d882751454004c22878af26.jpg)

Assigning equal weight to cumulative moves in real rates and FX, monetary conditions are not just loose, but have actually become looser over the course of this year (Figure 1). The slight rise in real policy rates (following a decline in headline inflation and exit from NIRP in March) has been more than

offset by continued real exchange rate depreciation.

For us, the takeaway at this juncture is twofold. First, sustained yen depreciation can continue to form one input into the BoJ policy path through its link with inflation. Lagged passthrough from currency moves into input prices can push up CPI inflation, partly reflecting firms<sup>’</sup> increasing ability to pass these costs into end prices. We thus remain comfortable in forecasting a policy rate of 50bp by year-end, above market pricing and probably ahead of where officials are signalling. We also project a tightening path for the BoJ that will extend gradually through early 2025.

Second, we continue to think that sustained rises in wage growth will be critical in bolstering domestic consumption and, in turn, holding up growth. The question is whether currency depreciation is helping or hindering. While a weak yen is contributing to stickier inflation, crimping real wages, it has also been boosting yen-denominated income from overseas. This is important, because real domestic demand has typically followed the broad contours of growth in nominal income from overseas, proxied by exports and investment income receipts (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Japan monetary conditions proxy
Figure 2: Japan nominal income and real domestic demand
![](images/d47fcc854d7594a2cda8e601483f0a12c8edeb8e473b154d32a033277716931d.jpg)

After some slowing through 2H23, the lagged effect of yen depreciation has the potential to bolster economy-wide corporate profit growth, providing a positive impulse on real domestic demand via household spending and business investment. The key here is for corporates to take solid profit growth and boost labor income through higher wages, a process that has been gradual thus far. In this context, we look for next week<sup>’</sup>s April employers<sup>’</sup> survey to confirm sustained rises in scheduled wage growth.

## Spending data treading water

Retail sales disappointed, falling 1.2%m/m, sa, in March, after a 1.7% rise in February. Despite the trend improvement in consumer sentiment, backed by a combination of falling inflation and rising wage expectations, consumer spending remained anemic in 1Q24, moving sideways after a 3.4%q/q, saar, drop in 4Q22. We expect 1Q GDP-based real consumption to mark the fourth consecutive quarterly fall of -0.5%q/q, saar, but the data still suggest some downside risk (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Japan Retail sales and consumer sentiment
![](images/3e2ce895ad6dbb27d1d7a7bee26f94596783772809e848d4db791442ac00a570.jpg)
2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 2025 Source: METI, Cabinet Office, J.P. Morgan

Details suggest general weakness in domestic consumption. Although sales for inbound consumption and to high income households, such as department store sales and luxury goods sales, have recorded strong gains, sales for others, such as supermarket and convenience store sales, remained weak. Although expectations are rising for higher wages, households appear to have remained cautious in increasing consumption due to real income drags that have lasted for two years.

## Goods cycle poised for midyear upturn

Japan<sup>’</sup>s March industrial production rose 3.8%m/m sa, in line with our forecast (4%) but a touch stronger than consensus expectations for a 3.3% gain. Given a very weak run through the early part of the year, output contracted 19.7%3m/3m saar, rounding off a soggy quarter for Japan<sup>’</sup>s goods sector that was depressed by the dual shocks of earthquake-related disruption and auto plant shutdowns.

Manufacturers are turning increasingly optimistic into 2Q, however. Producers expect an over-4% monthly rise in both April and May, implying a sharp acceleration in momentum through midyear (Figure 4). Even after controlling for the statistical upside bias in these projections, output is likely to inflect meaningfully higher this quarter. Details of the March report suggest this recovery is already underway. Both auto and electronics output printed solid over-9%m/m sa rises, with the latter pushed higher by a 13.7%m/m sa pop in semiconductor output (Figure 5). Chip production is running at decade highs, reflecting tailwinds from a regional tech cycle upturn and the more durable impact of an expansion in domestic production facilities.

Figure 4: Japan Manufacturing output %3m/3m saar, dashed line is manufacturers' projection
![](images/057c3342d4e37255850945241fe24d71355a1ffe072fc64685e1d5dc54fa97ca.jpg)
Figure 5: Manufacturing output by sector

![](images/ad9ee92592a9d5080d303faed5cb302d7d24f8ea714a486f253983651d1cdb81.jpg)

Despite several months of manufacturing weakness through late 2023 and into 2024, sentiment data have held up. But March data suggest some initial negative spillovers into the labor market. The unemployment rate has ticked up a couple of tenths from its cycle lows, holding steady at 2.6% in March, and measures of tightness have also moderated a touch from their 2023 extremes. While the labor market remains tight from a multi-year perspective, March data suggest a fall in manufacturing-related employment was the main drag on hiring through 1Q (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Employment by industry
![](images/cb8545e3c88544694b93f56f390a8abd9d792cabcaf36670d4d893e312bb57a0.jpg)

1. The DI asks whether a respondent thinks that now is a good time to purchase durables

April consumer sentiment disappointed, with the headline moving down to 38.3 from 39.5 in March. All sub-indices marked monthly declines. Among them, the timing to buy durable goods DI and the standard of living DI fell the most, with 2.2pts and 1.4pt falls, respectively. On the other hand, the labor market DI and the income growth DI stayed close to the post-pandemic highs, even after modest declines in April, reflecting the recent rising prospects for higher wages. We think today<sup>’</sup>s result showed households<sup>’</sup> rising concern about the trend weakening of the yen, and expect this to drive the BoJ to proceed with policy rate normalization modestly faster than currently indicated by the BoJ officials.

Source: ADA, JCSA, JDSA, JFA, JLM, VMA, Markit, METI, MHLW, MILT,MoF, Reuters, Statistics Bureau, J.P. Morgan forecasts

## Canada

• Real GDP grew 0.2% in February as we expected

Net trade tracking a flat contribution to real growth in 1Q24

• Small businesses<sup>’</sup> share of employment has been trending down since the pandemic

This week<sup>’</sup>s monthly real GDP and merchandise trade reports provided updated guidance on our tracking estimate of 1Q24 real GDP growth. Balancing the risks, we maintain our 2.0%ar forecast. February real GDP increased 0.2%, following a 0.5% gain in January (revised lower). Services-producing industries (+0.2%) led the growth for a second month fueled by gains in transportation and warehousing. Goodsproducing real output was essentially unchanged as the extraction sector expanded while real manufacturing output was weaker in February. Statistics Canada<sup>’</sup>s advance information on March indicates that real GDP was essentially unchanged. Data in hand reveals that the services sector was responsible for all of the real output growth in 1Q24 while real goods output was a slight drag on growth. Growth was robust at the the start of the first quarter but underlying momentum in some of the key activity indicators was easing late in the quarter (Figure 1). We are forecasting a deceleration in growth in the current quarter to 0.75%ar.

Figure 3: Employment share %, both scales
Figure 1: Real activity indicators
![](images/b1c03314c3f663db1ddf3e003bb56621529efd02d2a5728d1183dfdc045bda57.jpg)

## Net trade about neutral for 1Q24 real GDP growth

This week's March report wrapped up the merchandise trade data for 1Q24. In real terms, merchandise exports were about flat in 1Q24 while merchandise imports rebounded 2.4%ar. Declines observed in many export product categories were offset by higher exports of metal products driven by explosive growth in gold exports in February. Import volume growth was more broadly based across the major categories in 1Q, although volume imports of motor vehicles and parts were down for a second consecutive quarter. The trade surplus with the U.S. was a good bit weaker compared with the fourth quarter (Figure 2). The trade dynamics suggest the contribution to real GDP in 1Q24 from net real trade was about neutral, below our estimate before this week<sup>’</sup>s data. However, the general ebb in real exports in the first quarter (amid some volatility over the three months of the quarter) alongside a rebound in real imports suggests a stronger rebuild in inventories.

Figure 2: Trade balance with the U.S.
![](images/09344360f4dfadfa1d09800497f7ccb27e539c2ab488db5164db3c2721a591f4.jpg)

## No matter your size, you can do big things

In anticipation of next week<sup>’</sup>s April Labor Force Survey report, we pull back the lens and look at jobs growth since the pandemic by employer size. Small establishments (those employing fewer than 100 workers) have historically played an important role in job creation. And Canada is a country of small businesses. As of December 2022, there were 1.22 million employer businesses in Canada. Of these, 1.2 million (97.8%) were small businesses, 23,395 (1.9%) were mediumsized businesses, and 3,128 (0.3%) were large businesses. Small businesses have been navigating a challenging environment since the pandemic. The numbers show that the relative importance of small firms in total employment has been trending down during the last four years. Small firms accounted for \~62% of employment in recent months, a notable step down compared with 66% historically.The decline in small firms<sup>’</sup> share of employment is mainly associated with an increase in the relative importance of large-sized companies.Those companies saw their share of employment rise from an average of 34% historically to 38%.

![](images/5c7dc87cf7d45c0d7c9c94365fda620f84ef056dda7c3a79a87399e850685bb3.jpg)

## Mexico

• Banxico to hold rates

• CPI: Services remain point of strain

• GDP looks set to accelerate markedly

• IP should gain a healthy 0.7%m/m

Having just dipped its toes into easing at the last meeting, we expect Banxico will pause next week. Several factors are likely weighing on the board<sup>’</sup>s mind. Certainly the repricing of Fed cuts has some bearing, but is far from the most relevant factor at this point of the cycle. We think that for now domestic conditions will dictate Banxico<sup>’</sup>s decisions; once the cycle progresses, relative monetary policy will gain importance, as rate differentials could become binding. But at this point, we think Banxico has its sights squarely on inflation, which is proving extremely stubborn in the last stretch back into the target range, particularly core inflation. Core goods disinflation has provided significant relief, but services inflation remains a key point of strain. Through the noise around Easter, Mar/Apr services ex. housing and education likely rose at a rapid 0.45%m/m, sa, or over 6.5%3m/3m, saar through April (Figure 1). In our view, Banxico<sup>’</sup>s concern is that if services inflation remains sticky and core goods price gains normalize, core inflation would return to an ascending trend. April CPI will be released next week and we look for headline inflation to rise two-tenths while core eases a tenth to 4.65%oya and 4.42%, respectively.

Figure 1: Core services ex. housing and education %3m/3m, saar
![](images/08904784a36b37f6c7a3db922695381e07745aaaf93d94a625088b1f1d11b349.jpg)

At the same time, this week<sup>’</sup>s GDP data were not as weak as we expected, rising 0.8%q/q, saar whereas we expected a 0.2% contraction. This helps dispel some of the concerns about the economy having lost underlying momentum. In fact, after a weak end to last year and the start of this one, February GDP surged 1.4%m/m, and the March GDP implicit in the flash 1Q GDP data points to another increase of 1.2% in the month. This would leave activity on a very strong footing going into the current quarter, with statistical carryover at a massive 4.8% annualized, posing clear upside risks to our 4.2%ar forecast for the quarter. Next week we will get the

March IP data, which we expect to show a 0.7%m/m jump in production, in line with what was assumed in the flash GDP. But importantly, we expect manufacturing to post yet another gain (Figure 2), reinforcing the sense that the sector might be finally lifting after more than a year of sluggishness.

Figure 2: Manufacturing production
![](images/6374c3869faa4ed951eb3f35cf9d86b6a4d216b288ca836cf7edc0d04bb8e1df.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan forecasts

## Seems GDP dip was transitory after all

The flash 1Q GDP print was indeed weak, but not as weak as expected. Whereas we looked for a 0.2%ar drop, GDP was up a modest 0.8%. This alone leads us to revise up our full-year growth forecast to 2.1%y/y from 1.9%. The weak momentum late last year and early into this one had raised concerns about the underlying state of the economy. For some time we argued that we did not think momentum was as weak as the data were implying and that a host of other indicators (surveys, employment, wages, balance sheets) suggested that activity should bounce back.

To this point, there is not a lot of detail in the flash release, but enough to feel upbeat about GDP growth in 2Q. The GDP proxy was up 1.4%m/m in February , and data implicit in this week<sup>’</sup>s 1Q GDP point to an additional 1.2% pop in the GDP proxy in March. What<sup>’</sup>s more, all sectors appear to have posted solid gains in March (Figure 3). We call for a 4.2% annualized jump in GDP in the current quarter, but, with this week<sup>’</sup>s data in hand, statistical carryover alone has GDP tracking a near-5% gain going into 2Q.

![](images/21d331a3400a6c217cad63241455081ea191659d21dc801bca5b836cfddd0c55.jpg)
Source: INEGI. March changes implied in 1Q GDP Flash release

The GDP proxy and quarterly GDP data need not track tick by tick, but they often do. There are no hard data yet for 2Q, or much for March. In coming weeks we will get IP, retail sales, services income, and finally the March GDP proxy. Also, this week brings the April PMIs and business surveys. We will wait to see whether these reports play out as implied in this week<sup>’</sup>s numbers, but there is clearly meaningful upside risk to our already-upbeat 4.2%ar 2Q GDP forecast.

## Brazil

• Government celebrates improved rating outlook

• Challenges, however, persist amid political tension...

• …and growing fiscal risks for the medium term

• COPOM to slow easing pace next week

During Labor Day celebrations, Moody<sup>’</sup>s raised the outlook on Brazil<sup>’</sup>s Ba2 sovereign rating to positive, based on reforms carried out over the years and their impact on growth. Finance Minister Haddad welcomed the news, highlighting that it is related to the efforts of the three branches of power to improve the economic, social, and environmental outlooks for the country (<sup>‘</sup>Para Haddad, Moody<sup>’</sup>s reconheceu <sup>‘</sup>mudança para melhor<sup>’</sup> das perspectivas econômicas do Brasil,' Valor Econômico, May 1).

Moody<sup>’</sup>s Samar Maziad pointed out that without better prospects for fiscal accounts, the agency could revise this stance (<sup>‘</sup>Podemos reaaliar direção para o Brasil sem melhora no fiscal,' Agência Estado, May 1). In our view, this is indeed quite a hurdle, as marginal fiscal results reinforce the medium-term risks, particularly due to strong growth of recurring expenditures (Figure 1). Between signs of political fatigue on efforts the raise revenues, little will to cut expenditures, and moderate growth, public debt seems poised to grow while primary results fall over 2%-pts short of what would be needed to achieve stability.

Figure 1: Federal expenditures
![](images/8163ef7728e5c1cd6e40777d4848ffdfac156d9e0f9e36e26be3467fde9e3984.jpg)

Though highlighted by BCB board members on more than one occasion, it is not only fiscal policy on which they favor a more cautious stance. Despite the forward guidance given at the last meeting that BCB would cut the policy rate by 50bp, we believe the central bank will opt to reduce the pace and ease only 25bp to 10.50% at next week<sup>’</sup>s meeting. Many COPOM members publicly acknowledged that the combination of global financial tightening and the government<sup>’</sup>s decision to reduce the 2025 primary target increases the uncertainty over the appropriate monetary policy stance, amid signs of tighter labor markets and concerns on what this

means for inflation. In this context, we have changed our call and look for three back-to-back 25bp cuts at the next meetings, with a terminal rate of 10%, instead of the 50bp in May and three other 25bp cuts we initially anticipated. But our confidence in this view is low due to the heightened uncertainty on the economic outlook.

In both the statement and the minutes of the next meeting, it will be important to read the extent of the divergences within the Board. We see increasing chances of a dissent vote in next week<sup>’</sup>s decision, given the contrasting forces of improving current core CPI, against the worries about future inflation dynamics on the deterioration of inflation and fiscal expectations, a tight labor market keeping services CPI elevated, and high rates abroad affecting the exchange rate. However, we anticipate a unanimous decision for a 25bp cut, seeing the potential divergences within the Board mostly around the views on guidance—if and when to finish the easing cycle— and balance of risks, rather than what was the most appropriate pace of cuts at this juncture.

In this sense, the focus likely will be on what the central bank signals about the next steps for monetary policy. There are arguments on both sides about the usage of forward guidance. Since 2016 BCB seldom has not given guidance for the next meeting but always has highlighted—though with different degrees of emphasis—its data dependency. We think the COPOM will keep the door open for a continuation of the cutting cycle with another 25bp cut at the June meeting but reiterating that the communication doesn<sup>’</sup>t constrain the central bank<sup>’</sup>s future decisions.

## CAD widens more than expected

The March current account deficit (CAD) came out at US\$4.6 billion, wider than our call of US\$3.3 billion with the surprise stemming from higher interest, profits and dividend payments, and crypto asset imports (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Cryptoasset net trade
![](images/ea4d14eaf7b150b76f359d2a48e0dd3ade19084ec9f319412c2f56068902fc70.jpg)

CAD moved up from 1.2% of GDP to 1.5%, driven by weakness in the trade balance in March. Foreign direct investment (FDI) was strong for the third consecutive month, registering inflows of US\$9.6 billion and moving up a tenth to 3% of GDP. All considered, we continue looking for the current account deficit to end the year around 1.4% of GDP.

## Solid IP and tight labor market

The unemployment rate fell two-tenths to a record low 7.4% (sa). Employment growth was widespread across sectors, but for the first time in nine months real wages decreased 0.2%m m, sa, with nominal wages decelerating from 9% to 8.4% and real wages down three-tenths to 4%oya.

Meanwhile, industrial production expanded 0.9%m/m, sa, below our call of 1.5% and consensus of 1.4%, but with a four-tenths upward revision to the February release. IP grew 1.2%q/q, saar in 1Q24, decelerating from more than 5%q/q, saar in 4Q23, but with all the quarterly weakness concentrated in January and in the mining sector, whereas manufacturing production grew in all months.

## Argentina

Lower House approved the Omnibus bill 2.0 and the Fiscal package; focus now turns to the Senate

• Through March, the BCRA bought US\$11.4bn in the official FX market...

• ...though accumulated US\$11.1bn in new commercial debt and issued Bopreal of US\$8.2bn

• Advancing toward release of capital controls is necessary to replenish reserves in the medium term

The ruling party managed to approve the Omnibus bill 2.0 and the Fiscal package in the Lower House, with the voting in general obtaining 142 and 140 positive votes, respectively. Unlike the first attempt, this time all the individual chapters and articles of both bills survived the vote, implying a strong (first) win for the government. Regarding the Omnibus bill 2.0, the most relevant chapters include a labor reform, the incentive regime for large investments, and the delegation of powers to the Executive Branch to modify, merge, or dissolve special-purpose federal government funds. They also allowed the privatization of 11 companies, including Aerolineas Argentinas, as well as telecommunications, train, and public utilities companies, among others. From a fiscal perspective, the most relevant changes approved by the Lower House involve a tax amnesty as well as the re-incorporation of the income tax (both measures jointly accounting for about 0.8% of GDP in new revenues in our estimates this year).

The focus now turns to the Senate, where both bills will start to be discussed likely next week (at the earliest by Thursday). In line with the process in the Lower House, the bills will first need to be approved at commission; then comes a general vote, and, if the bills are approved, lawmakers vote on each individual article. Thus, the Upper House vote will likely take place the week of May 20. Note that Union Por La Patria lawmakers account for 46% (33) of the Upper House and LLA+PRO+Radicales account for 37% (27 out of 72). Thus, the voting outcome is expected to be tighter than in the Lower House, but we still assign a high probability of the bill being approved in the Senate. That said, the risk is that the bill will suffer some modifications. According to the local press, there is still pushback at the Senate on the re-incorporation of the income tax and the increase in the tobacco tax approved in the Lower House. Note that if there are changes made at the Senate, then the bills go back to the Lower House for the ratification of those changes, which could delay the sanctioning of the law beyond May 25.

## Reserves accumulation, new imports debt

The cash-basis current account posted a US\$5.3bn surplus in 1Q24, the largest seen for a first quarter on the series records (dating back to 2003). When compared to the US\$4.9bn deficit reported in the same quarter last year, it implies a US\$10.2bn reversal. The strong reversal in the quarter has been driven by the cash-basis trade balance improvement, against the backdrop of new commercial (imports) debt accumulation.

Indeed, the cash-basis merchandise trade balance recorded a US\$9.4bn surplus in 1Q24, US\$9.1bn above the same period last year. Worth noting, the bulk of the improvement is explained by lower imports payments, down by US\$8.7bn when compared with the same period last year (Figure 1). Meanwhile, exports inflows were only up by US\$0.5bn YTD, though we see a further pickup in the months ahead amid the soy harvest season. Admittedly, the activity contraction has added downward pressure on imported quantities, explaining about half of the cash imports plunge YTD. Yet, note that, when looking at the accrued basis report, imports FOB have declined by 24.2%oya YTD, or US\$4.2bn. Thus, the plunge in cash imports has been also driven by the new import payment restrictions, as the BCRA only allows for the payment of imports in four installments, the flip-side of new commercial (imports) debt accumulation.

Figure 1: Import payments
![](images/aebeca96cf531d6fc88cd1234898b94a540cb59859deec5a0e3c960e5634f29d.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

The services balance also helps to explain the positive current account result YTD, with the services deficit narrowing by US\$1.9bn when compared to same quarter last year and standing close to a balance by March. This has been mainly explained by lower tourism outflows in 1Q24 on the back of the FX devaluation, as well as lower freight costs. Meanwhile, the income shortfall widened by US\$0.8bn on higher interest payments, to both the market and the IMF.

Against this backdrop, the BCRA has bought US\$15.1bn in reserves since December 10, consistent with a daily average of US\$160mn. In our estimates, net FX reserves stand now at -US\$5.0bn (note our estimate considers as hard-currency liabilities Bopreal series 2 amortizations for US\$1.7bn in the next 12 months and Treasury deposits for US\$2.0bn as of April 30. Meanwhile, USD liquid reserves stood at around US\$8.2bn (from almost null when the administration took

office). Of note, the government paid capital amortizations of US\$1.9bn to the IMF this week.

The administration<sup>’</sup>s import payments framework has had a strong impact on the ability to accumulate reserves. In our estimates, between December and March, new commercial debt has increased by US\$11.1bn. That compares to the central bank buying US\$11.4bn in the official FX market in the same period, and to US\$8.2bn in Bopreal issued through March (Figure 2). Thus, even though imports payments have started to normalize in March (almost 60% of total imports were paid), reserves accumulation so far has been explained by the increase of commercial debt, which exceeded Bopreal issuance in the same period. The BCRA announced recently that it is evaluating the possibility of issuing new Bopreal for companies that need to repatriate dividends to non-residents. While the stock of dividends trapped is unknown, the BCRA data show that between 2016 and 2019, repatriation of dividends and profits amounted to US\$7.3bn, compared to just US\$769mn between 2020 and 2023.

Tax revenues
Figure 2: BCRA reserves accumulation framework US\$bn
![](images/cdf3ff126d8913480dc25ec97c6bea1a89ad95aaf72bf0fd0d296fcacae79b2e.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

## Merits and drawbacks of the FX strategy

The FX gap has remained relatively stable on the quarter at around 20%-25%, narrowing substantially from around 200% when the new administration took office. The compression has been supported by the exporters<sup>’</sup> USD supply to the financial FX (20% of total exports sales), and the bulk of capital controls remaining in place. On the former, we estimate that there has been a supply of around US\$5.3bn in the parallel FX market coming from exports sales in between December and March (US\$60mn daily average). While containing the parallel FX, the flipside has been a lower level of FX purchases at the official FX market vis-à-vis a scenario without the 80/20% exporting scheme. Indeed, extending the blend FX framework through June would result in an additional US\$3bn reserves sacrifice, in our projections.

Against this backdrop, the BCRA has sustained a 2% monthly crawling peg, with average monthly inflation of 12.5% in the December-April period (April is JPM forecast). While the low crawling monthly rate has helped anchor short-term inflation expectations, it has also resulted in an REER appreciation of more than 40% since the December FX devaluation. Note the REER has converged to a level comparable to that of August <sup>’</sup>23 before the post-primaries FX adjustment. Meanwhile, the parallel FX has converged close to the importers FX level (the latter equivalent to the official exchange rate level +17.5% PAIS tax), which we view as a floor.

Independent of the “equilibrium REER level<sup>”</sup> (always a tricky variable to gauge, particularly amid regime change), REER appreciation is not without costs, as it could eventually conspire against price effects and growth, impacting incentives of exporters and importers of both goods and services. That could in turn weigh on the ability to continue accumulating FX reserves once the seasonal inflows from soy exports are left behind. Note that, absent changes to the current policy template, we estimate the REER would revisit November 2023 levels by July (assuming average monthly inflation of 7% in May-June). Admittedly, a faster disinflation process in 2Q than we are envisioning could buy more time, slowing down the real appreciation trend. But in any case, as we emphasized in our recent note, advancing toward the release of capital controls in a prompt manner is a necessary condition to secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in the medium term.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: March headline activity lost 0.7%m/m, sa, a payback after two very strong months

We expect 2Q24 to decelerate after the strong start of the year; 2024 GDP projection kept at +2.7%y/y

Colombia: The Board, by majority, cut the policy rate by 50bp to 11.75%

We expect another 50bp cut at the upcoming meeting by June-end, and an acceleration in the easing in 2H24

## Chile: 1Q24 activity shines

The monthly headline activity index slipped by 0.7%m/m, sa, in March, on the heels of the strong 0.9% recorded in February (revised higher from +0.8%). A more granular analysis shows services, industry, and commerce down on the month, by 0.4%, 1.3% and 0.1%, respectively.

Despite the (expected) correction lower in March, headline activity gave a stellar performance in 1Q24, more than recovering from the 4Q23 pothole. Indeed, upon revisions, headline activity ran at 8.4%3m/3m, saar, a strong acceleration from the +0.4% observed in 4Q23. Thus by March headline activity gained a remarkable 2.3%sa, compared to December<sup>’</sup>s level.

Activity increased 0.8%oya in March. But adjusted for calendar effects (three fewer workdays this year) the growth rate climbed to +2.1%oya (+2.2%oya in 1Q24).

As usual, we focus on ex. mining activity, the most useful indicator from a cyclical standpoint. Ex. mining contracted 0.6 %m/m, sa, on the heels of a 0.7% gain in February. Like headline activity, despite the March contraction, ex. mining gained 5.6%3m/3m, saar in 1Q24, accelerating from the 2.3% observed in 4Q23, and proving the strongest quarterly expansion pace since late 2021. Finally, always choppy mining production also dropped on the month, by 1.3 %sa, but gained by a whopping 30.4%3m/3m, saar (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile economic activity
![](images/ecce6267d4c5272a8e59a092cbb6e4b5501155b5365655cb5e6d616554fa7ab1.jpg)
Source CBC and J.P. Morgan

In all, despite the March contraction proving a bit larger than what we had forecasted, the sequential performance of the monthly activity indicator proved a stronger, at +8.4%ar versus our 8.0% projection. Ahead, we expect a substantial deceleration in 2Q24 GDP, mainly on mining activity, to +0.5%q/q, saar. We maintain our 2024 GDP projection at +2.7%y/y (+3.3%Q4/Q4).

## Colombia: Slow and steady wins the race

The Board, by majority, cut the policy rate by 50bp to 11.75%, as widely expected. Like the prior meeting, five directors out of seven voted for a 50bp cut, maintaining the majority block in place since the easing cycle started. The other two directors, who have consistently advocated for a more aggressive easing pace, were split between a 75bp and 100bp cut. Ahead, the Board remains data dependent with respect to the easing pace.

On the cyclical discussion, the statement notes that headline inflation in March eased to 7.4%oya and inflation without food or regulated items to 6.8%oya, consolidating the downward trend observed since 2023 (see note). Also, inflation expectations as per the BanRep survey remained stable at 4.6% in one year and 3.5% in two, while those derived from public debt markets mostly decreased, although their levels remain above the goal. Another highlight from the statement is the reference to the persistent and still above-target US core inflation, which coupled with the tight labor market and upward revisions to economic growth, led to higher mediumand long-term interest rates in global markets. Finally, the Staff revised 2024 GDP higher, to 1.4%y/y (from 1.1%, previously).

When it comes to the path ahead, in our base case we assume the Board continues the easing cycle, as the ex-ante real rate remains high. The decline in the next 12-m inflation expectations has roughly aligned with the reduction of the nominal policy rate, so that the ex ante real rate is currently just 50bp below the level observed at the beginning of the easing cycle (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Colombia monetary policy rate
![](images/b72bb8ffdb0554f51f03ec01603fdf3bca780f9aad360ec91955f7fd81ff0d08.jpg)

Retail sales

In our base case we expect another 50bp policy rate cut at the upcoming meeting scheduled for the end of June. We do expect an acceleration of the easing cycle in 2H24, assuming the Fed starts to adjust the policy rate lower. We project the policy rate level for Dec-24 at 8.5%. On the terminal rate, we maintain the level at 6.25%, due to a higher r\* than what Ban-Rep estimates due to fiscal risk (see note for discussion).

## United Kingdom

• No change in rates expected from the MPC next week...

• ...but vote likely to show more dovish dissent

• We expect signalling for a 3Q start to cuts

We expect no change in rates from the BoE next week. We assume Ramsden will join Dhingra in dissenting for a cut, in a slightly closer 7-2 vote. Even that is not entirely clear, but it does seem the natural implication of his recent speech. Although other dovish dissent is possible, its not obvious who that would come from or indeed why a switch should happen now.

Near-term services inflation and private sector pay growth are tracking a little above the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecasts. And despite a wobble in some of the jobs indicators lately, revisions shortly after the BoE<sup>’</sup>s February MPR mean the LFS unemployment rate is still on track to come in below the Bank<sup>’</sup>s near-term forecast. We also expect the BoE to revise up its 1Q GDP forecast from an annualised 0.4% to around 1%, and this should add to the sense that the jobs market will at least stabilise this year (Table 1).

## Table 1: BoE forecasts

%, based on market rates assumption and modal forecasts, unless stated

Source: BoE, J.P. Morgan

The MPC will again want to hint at an easing later this year, as the inflation problem has become less acute. At the February report we inferred the message to be a 2H24 start. Next week, the BoE will not want to rule out June, given there is a lot of data to come before then that could tip the balance. But, if we are right on the vote, we don<sup>’</sup>t think the Bank will indicate any particular urgency. We think it will aim to leave the impression that the first cut is likely around August time, but with a possibility of June.

Barring significant data surprises it seems unlikely that Mann, Haskel, Greene or Pill would support a June cut. So strong signalling about a move at that meeting next week seems less likely. It would require a clearer shift in thinking from Breeden, Bailey and Broadbent, as they are the remaining members who would be required to form a majority for an easing (assuming Dhingra and Ramsden dissent next week).

Bailey and Broadbent have never voted differently from Pill in the 21 meetings they have shared. Some have interpreted Bailey<sup>’</sup>s Washington remarks as dovish, but a close inspection of his words shows he has not really said anything new. He described the inflation process as “pretty much on track<sup>”</sup> with the view laid out in early February.

Members have been at pains to argue they are not following the Fed, and do generally seem happy to signal that an easing shouldn<sup>’</sup>t be too far off given relative demand weakness in Europe. From this perspective, it might seem logical for the MPC<sup>’</sup>s language to evolve as the time approaches. Recently it has only said it will “keep under review<sup>”</sup> how long rates should remain at the current level. However, there appears limited value in making a more discreet change at this stage, i.e. adopting a very explicit easing bias.

Doing so wouldn<sup>’</sup>t be a necessary precursor to cutting rates. And with several hawkish views on the committee, as well as mixed signs of progress in the data recently, a significant change in guidance probably wouldn<sup>’</sup>t be wise. The possibility of stronger growth, persistent underlying inflation and, yes, even Fed actions, could yet cause the BoE to get cold feet in the coming weeks. So there is benefit to remaining ambiguous, and its current guidance offers this in abundance.

The BoE will be looking at a market rate path that shows just under two cuts for this year and rates slightly above 4% by the end of 2025. That is a lot different from February, when there had been four cuts priced for this year and an end-2025 level of 3.5%. Part of the higher rates profile is justified by reduced recession risks, although we think the MPC will still view the current 2025 profile as a bit too high.

Higher expected rates are likely to mechanically lower the future growth and inflation path, but the February MPR<sup>’</sup>s inflation projections implied that the MPC didn<sup>’</sup>t agree with the market path at the time, and so we wouldn<sup>’</sup>t place much weight on downward revisions stemming from this source. We would expect the BoE to show inflation at target in two years, broadly endorsing current market expectations for a cut around August (with some chance of June). Beyond then, inflation is likely to be shown close to but a little below target if, as we expect, the MPC views the current market rate profile as too high.

## Sweden and Norway

• We expect the Riksbank to cut the policy rate next week...

• ...but close the door for another cut in June

• Norges Bank to cut rate in December (our prior forecast was September)

Data out of Sweden in recent weeks have almost all been supportive of lower interest rates. The GDP indicator for the first quarter was down -0.4%q/q, saar (-1.1%oya), the fourth consecutive quarter with contraction. The fall was a surprise given strong outcomes in the monthly indicator. Given the quarterly GDP indicator is prone to large revisions we only adjust our 1Q forecast down to -0.1%q/q, saar. We do not think the report will have much influence on the Riksbank<sup>’</sup>s decision next week, but it could temper members<sup>’</sup> description that domestic demand has been firmer than envisaged.

The LFS unemployment rate also jumped 0.5%pts to 8.6%, sa in March. This was well-above expectations (JPM & consensus: 8.2%). While this measure of unemployment is notoriously volatile, an increase of 0.5%-pts is unusual and rarely seen out of recessions. The jump was driven by a 0.6% surge in the labour force with the participation ratio increasing from 75.2% to 75.6% and employment was flat.

Finally, the Swedish NIER survey price indicators, which have been highlighted by Riksbank members as important for upcoming decisions, dropped below their long-run average in April, consistent with inflation at 2% or below (Figure 1). The headline ETI index on the other hand rose 1.9pts to 95.0, suggesting GDP growth at 0.6%q/q, saar. Hence, this is supportive of our forecast Sweden will move out of recession in 2Q. Consumer confidence increased 1.3pts to 88.9. Whilst still below long-run average, it has rebounded significantly.

Figure 1: Core inflation and retailers' industry price expectations
![](images/d038a260c9c7c7f7ce594b16131aae19ea889a26ad0784a2c61a8ebfc350457f.jpg)
Offering hope of recovery the manufacturing PMI increased 1.0pts to 51.4 in April. Details were positive as orders and employment improved, although employment remained

below 50. On the negative side, the price index soared 6.6pts to 53.4, likely due to metal prices and weaker SEK. We do, however, not think it will have much impact on the Riksbank decision next week as members traditionally put more weight on the NIER price indicators.

On the Riksbank, we expect a 25bps cut to 3.75%. The main argument against this call is the weak krona. We think the currency will have some hawkish influence via forward guidance as we think the door for another cut in June will be closed. As a result, we change our forecast to expect the next cut in September, lowering the number of rate cuts in 2024 from five to four. We still anticipate a policy rate at 2% by the end of 2025.

Looking ahead, we see core inflation at 3.0%oya in April, narrowing the gap to the Riksbank<sup>’</sup>s forecast a notch to 0.3%-pt. This is based on some payback in food prices (which were lower-than-usual in March because of the early Easter), a weaker SEK, and a rise in global commodity prices. Tempering upside surprises is the sharp drop in the NIER pricing indicators.

## Norges Bank to cut rates in December

Norges Bank changed its forward guidance at this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, saying the policy rate would probably stay at current levels longer than previously envisaged. While the Committee seldom changes guidance at interim meetings, we had seen an elevated risk of such an outcome due to a weaker NOK and a high wage settlement in the industrial sector. As a consequence, we have changed our forecast to a first cut in December instead of September.

Looking at data releases ahead, we expect GDP to have contracted 0.1%m/m in March, in line with our model on available hard data, but below Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast (0.0%). For 1Q as a whole we see GDP up 0.5%q/q, saar. On inflation, we expect core to come in at 4.1%oya in April, thereby widening the gap to Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast to 0.4%-pt (Figure 2).

## Emerging Europe

• Russia: Growth cooled in March, but 1Q was strong

• Poland: CPI undershoots despite VAT increase

• Czech Republic: a hawkish cut

• Türkiye: Core inflation likely peaked in April

## Russia: Strong 1Q, softer March

The theme of resilience remains a constant in Russian macro discussions — resilience to war and confidence shocks, sanctions and, more lately, to significant monetary tightening. Indeed, we have continued to raise our growth forecasts in response to incoming (strong) data, even as we keep anticipating a loss of momentum in coming quarters in response to a less supportive fiscal policy and restrictive monetary policy. We currently expect GDP to expand 3.3% in 2024, following a 3.6% increase in 2023.

That said, March hard activity data did soften relative to prior months. The index of basic sectors, which covers more than half of the economy, slid 0.1%m/m, sa, we estimate. That follows 1.3%, 0.3% and 2.2% increases in previous three months, and tracks almost 9%, saar, gain for 1Q24. We think 1Q GDP is tracking around 5.25%q/q, saar (5.3%oya), with risks probably skewed slightly to upside. We would treat the March signal on cooling with caution, as most other activity indicators, including business and consumer surveys, have remained fairly strong through March-April. Yet, our sense is that once monetary and fiscal impulses make their way through the system the slowdown will ensue, and, given the usual lags, it should probably happen at around mid-year.

Industrial output added 0.1%m/m, sa, in March, on our estimates, building on the strong gains of the prior couple of months. Mining output decreased 1.1%, while manufacturing added 1.4%. Within manufacturing, strength was concentrated in capex- and military-intensive industries, while heavyweigh oil refining and metals saw output declines. Other production-side indicators were mixed. Construction works were flat on the month, transport turnover dropped 1.7%m/m, sa, while wholesale trade turnover rose 0.4%.

Consumer demand saw a slight set-back in March, the first since November last year. Retail sales gained a very modest (by recent standards) 0.2%m/m, sa, while consumption of services dropped 0.5%. This seems somewhat inconsistent with the recent re-acceleration in consumer credit and strong income gains, so our inclination is to partially downplay the March weakness in consumer demand even as we do expect slowdown later in the year.

The labor market remained very tight, with the unemployment rate sliding to 2.7% sa in the latest print. Both the labor force and employment edged up a tick last month. Nominal wage growth accelerated to 19.3%oya in February, with private sector wages running above 20%. To be sure, the leap year effect must have inflated the February numbers, but sequential momentum in (nominal) wages was nevertheless very strong at above 20% ar in the first two months of the year.

## Poland: CPI undershoots despite VAT

According to the preliminary release, inflation stood below the NBP<sup>’</sup>s 2.5% target in April, despite a reinstatement of food VAT at 5% (from 0%). The data show CPI increased to just 2.4%oya (Figure 1), from 2%, once again somewhat below market expectations (Consensus: 2.5%oya, JPM: 2.6%).

![](images/3068607eeea9ef2e4d6f10721e1c755273324006cb7a591f56cc71320c988cd3.jpg)

![](images/bda78cd6d72517819c115bd8ce31d72131ca9b6b66880d4a0634bdc251cd8a4a.jpg)

Food prices were the key mover in April, with a tax-driven 2.1%m/m increase (Figure 2). Although a sizeable move, this is below our 3.1%m/m assumption, and suggests retailers absorbed a larger part of the 5%-pt VAT increase than what we expected, corroborating the anecdotes about “discount wars<sup>”</sup> between big Polish food retailers. Its hard to know how sustainable this retailer margin reduction can, versus how much of it is a PR exercise to be gradually reversed over coming months (with delayed pass-through of the VAT reinstatement into prices).

Core CPI is not published with the flash release, but if we mechanically extract the contributions of non-core items, we are left with a residual which suggests core prices were up 0.575%m/m, half a decimal above our forecast (0.525%m/m). Given the preliminary nature of these data, plus the lack of decimal precision, the estimates have some uncertainty. If confirmed however, they imply core CPI falling to 4%oya in April (Figure 3), from 4.6%, close to our forecast (3.9%).

Figure 3: Poland Core CPI
![](images/712bf1de3be0994b707a0dc7878bb4736814d6d938b5354a8d597df7070041dd.jpg)
Source: GUS, J.P. Morgan

The NBP has been pursuing the most hawkish stance across CEE central banks, suggesting it could be the only one to remain on hold the remainder of the year. But the activity data is not strong, and inflation has been much lower than expected. In its latest inflation report, the NBP published two alternative forecasts, one where “CPI shielding measures<sup>”</sup> were extended (Food VAT at 0%, frozen utilities), and one higher inflation outcome where they weren<sup>’</sup>t extended. In the first one, which assumes Food VAT frozen at 0%,the NBP forecasted CPI to average 3.2%oya in 1Q and 2.5% in 2Q. We now know CPI in 1Q came at only 2.8%, and the April read ing puts it on track to repeat the 2.8% in 2Q, just three decimals above the NBP<sup>’</sup>s “best case scenario<sup>”</sup> (even though VAT was up). Core inflation was lower than the NBP forecast by two decimals in 1Q and is on track to be much better in 2Q (we predict 3.9%oya in 2Q, vs the NBP<sup>’</sup>s 4.5%).

By mid-year, if utility price adjustments are equally on the modest side, we believe the NBP can soften the stance, provided the ECB/Fed are on, or about to be on, some easing path. Therefore we maintain our call for some small easing steps at the end of this year, with two 25bp cuts delivered in each of November and December. Risks to our call are tilted towards rates on hold all year.

## Czech Republic: Hawkish cut

At this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, the CNB decided to cut the 2-week repo rate by another 50bps to 5.25% (Figure 4), as expected by us and the market. Markets priced an outside chance (around 15%) of a smaller 25bp move, and some influential board members indeed voiced indecision between 25 or 50 ahead of the meeting. But ultimately, the decision was backed with the board's full unanimity (7-0), whereas we had expected one or two votes for 25bps.

Figure 4: CNB interest rate corridor
![](images/6398aee2d08e7f56074da783f5a356713ea39e7b61a62bcb632d0886e6ba6b1e.jpg)

As in the previous rate cuts, the CNB presented a message which is unquestionably hawkish, arguably the most hawkish so far. The first key piece was Governor Michl<sup>’</sup>s presser, where he argued that the approach to further rate cuts would be very careful and the cycle could be halted/interrupted should pro-inflationary risks materialize. Michl stressed that the board still sees a higher rate path than the official forecast, but the gap between the two has narrowed. Concrete guidance was absent, with Michl explaining that the board will decide based on new data from meeting to meeting.

Table 1: CNB Forecast summary
<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td colspan="2">2024</td><td colspan="2">2025</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Domestic variables</td><td>Old</td><td>New</td><td>Old</td><td>New</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6"></td></tr><tr><td>GDP growth</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>0.6</td><td>1.4</td><td>2.4</td><td>2.7</td></tr><tr><td>CPI</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.0</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>CPI</td><td>(4q)</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.5 25.1</td><td>2.1</td><td>1.9</td></tr><tr><td>EUR/CZK</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>24.6</td><td></td><td>24.3</td><td>24.8</td></tr><tr><td>EUR/CZK</td><td>(4q)</td><td>24.4</td><td>25.0</td><td>24.3</td><td>24.7</td></tr><tr><td>3m Pribor</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>4.0</td><td>5.0</td><td>2.6</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>3m Pribor</td><td>(4q)</td><td>2.9</td><td>4.3</td><td>2.7</td><td>3.1</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">External assumptions</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area CPI</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>1.8</td><td>2.6</td><td>1.8</td><td>2.2</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area PPI</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>-3.5</td><td>-5.4</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area GDP</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.5</td><td>1.4</td><td>1.5</td></tr><tr><td>3m Euribor</td><td>(avg.)</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.5</td><td>2.4</td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>EUR/USD</td><td></td><td>1.09</td><td>1.09</td><td>1.12</td><td>1.11</td></tr><tr><td>Brent</td><td>(avg.) (avg.)</td><td>76.1</td><td>84.2</td><td>73.1</td><td>79.1</td></tr></table>

Source: CNB; J.P. Morgan

The second key piece of the CNB<sup>’</sup>s hawkish message is the new forecast (Table 1). Despite a massive downside surprise to CPI in 1Q24 (CPI was 2.1%oya vs 2.9% forecasted, core CPI was 2.8%oya vs 3.5% predicted) the projection shows a quite similar CPI path, on the back of higher assumption for inflation abroad, higher Brent and weaker FX. Crucially, the 3m-Pribor projection was revised massively higher, by 135bps to 4.3% at the end of 2024 (2.9% previously), which brings the CNB<sup>’</sup>s projection roughly in line with ours.

We had expected a signal for an imminent slowdown in the easing pace, either through a vote split or through verbal communication. Absent that, we cannot rule out another 50bps in June. But we still think its more likely the pace shifts lower to 25bps, due to the high core inflation prints in February/March, especially within services. The repricing of DM central bank easing is not such a bind for the CNB as it is for other EMs, but likely inspires increased caution nonetheless. We expect that in the remainder of the year inflation will oscillate between 2%oya and 3.2% (Figure 5), ending the year with a 2.8% average in 4Q24. This will create space for rates to fall towards the 4% area, which has been our call for a while, while remaining in real terms above the 1% traditionally assumed by the CNB.

## Türkiye: Core inflation likely peaked in April

Headline CPI inflation came in at 3.2% m/m in April, slightly lower than expectations. CPI inflation increased to 69.8% oya in April, from 68.5% in March mainly driven by unfavorable base effects in energy and core prices. Services prices rose by a high 4.2% m/m in April, leading over-year-ago services inflation to all-time high of 97% mainly due to transportation prices. Core goods prices rose 3% m/m, pushing up overyear-ago inflation to 57.1% in April, from 56.5% in March, mainly due to clothing prices. As a result, core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose by a high 3.6% m/m, leading oya core inflation to 75.8% in April (75.2% oya in March, Figure 5).

![](images/c15010aef1f91a3c8e166b6fc50774aea3b63f43d4f319937d82b0ca166411de.jpg)
Core inflation momentum (3m change, saar, %) improved in April thanks to both services and core goods prices. Core goods inflation momentum slightly fell in April. Services inflation momentum started to fall from its elevated levels (Figure 6).

![](images/0d3c3eb52c1466771092b9a4a0418738adebc4d696e28a04f141a6b2c7de2aaf.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

Monthly headline and core inflation likely peaked in April. The Turkish lira performed well in April and early May on the back of de-dollarization among Turkish residents and foreign inflows. We expect monthly inflation to come down in the summer months with core goods prices driven by real lira appreciation and the slowdown in the economic activity. We believe the worst part of monthly inflation prints is behind us.

Headline over-year-ago inflation is likely to peak at 75% in May (previously 76%) owing to unfavourable base effects driven by natural gas prices (Figure 7). We revise down our year-end inflation forecast to 43.5% (previously 44.7%) as we now assume more gradual electricity and natural gas price hikes over 2H24 and 2025. We will monitor to what extent real appreciation of lira will offset the effects of realised wage hikes coupled with expected electricity and natural gas price hikes in July or later.

Figure 7: CPI forecasts
![](images/6ea418b6b9287895563de33f4af64721c3a2fc80e23d611c30bd04030f9712b0.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

## South Africa

• Election surveys show surprise recovery in estimated ANC support

This reduces probabilities on tail-risk scenarios, boosting our base case

Fiscal data confirm earlier positive signal of a small beat of the budget target, rather than slippage

• Trade surplus of 2.4% of GDP in 1Q; C/A deficit likely at 0.9%

The trend of declining ANC support, as measured in survey polls, has now halted and is showing tentative signs of a recovery ahead of national elections on 29 May. In particular, the recent high-frequency polls of the social research foundation (SRF) now capture ANC support at 42.3% (as measured at end-April), up from 37.1% in mid-April based on the 66% turnout scenario. The estimated support is even marginally higher at 43.6% in the 58% turnout scenario. This is relevant as in the event of ANC support at 43%-44% (and higher) we think a coalition with minor opposition parties becomes the base case scenario, in contrast to the scenario of ANC below 42%.

In addition, the updated IPSOS poll surprised with ANC support at 40.2%, almost unchanged from the February estimate of 40.5%. As the earlier poll had excluded the MK party in its fieldwork (which now is estimated to 8.4% support, at the expense of the ANC), the newer poll was widely anticipated to see ANC support reported lower. The 40.2% estimate is, however, not a projection for the election polls. While the release is not clear on this, it has not yet adjusted for a turnout scenario, which in its February poll had lifted the projected ANC support to 45%. We think the turnout adjustment for the recent report will also be released in time, but perhaps by a modestly lesser 3%-4%pts to about 43.5%.

Our earlier note on survey polls inferred ANC support at 42% when adjusted for voter turnout, but with a margin of error (based on prior survey cycles) that would put the outcome in the range of 42% to 45%. Our base case therefore is that the ANC will gain close enough to 45% of voter support to enable a coalition with minor opposition parties, such as the IFP, PA, and GOOD, to bring it over the 50% mark. For now, however, these minor opposition parties have largely denied their interest to join such a coalition, which we don<sup>’</sup>t find unusual until elections have passed. The recent IPSOS and SRF polls reduce the probabilities of the tail risk scenarios (i.e. a coalition with either the EFF, MK or alternatively the DA).

## A fiscal feat in 1Q24

The fiscal data for FY23/24, ending in March, confirmed the earlier positives signs from the cashflow data that the budget target was marginally exceeded. The fiscal deficit narrowed to 4.6% of GDP (Budget: 4.7%) with a sharp contraction in spending last quarter (Figure 1). Revenue was marginally ahead of expectations, likely due to measures to improved tax administration. The tight expenditure target seemed a stretch as recent as January, when spending was well ahead of the target and risked slippage of 0.3%-0.5%pts of GDP.

Figure 1: Revenue ratio and rolling fiscal deficit
![](images/cf6d4c4c343930bdd3affd164b9cf5ed1dd75f3d7fcd62cef5c6b01b8d729303.jpg)

However, a sharp slowing in core spending to 0.1%oya in January and February (from 7.2%oya April to December) with a 9% contraction in March resulted in spending just a touch ahead of the Budget. We think this shows that the perseverance of National Treasury and the better messaging has paid off and is helping to reinforce the fiscal framework. We had already marginally improved the fiscal forecast following encouraging signals in early April and we expect a fiscal deficit of 4.8% in FY24/25 (Treasury: 4.3%), equivalent to 5.7% when including the Eskom support package.

## Bumper 1Q24 trade surplus

Despite a swing to a deficit in March, the trade balance recorded a strong 2.4% of GDP surplus in 1Q. The trade balance recorded an R3.1bn sa deficit in March as exports (-14.9%m/m) fell faster than imports (-2.5%). The decline in exports was broad based in March, which likely reflects a payback from the unusual gains in January and February amid thawing logistical bottlenecks. As a result, we now estimate a trade surplus of 2.4% of GDP last quarter (from 1.2% in 4Q) which is larger than the 0.8% we had initially projected. All told, the C/A deficit probably narrows from 2.3% in 4Q23 to 0.9% of GDP in 1Q24 (vs. our earlier projections of an expansion to -2.5%), which in turn trims the <sup>’</sup>24 shortfall to 2.2% (from 2.6%).

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s retail sales were weaker than anticipated in March, declining 0.4%m/m sa

We expect the RBA to remain on hold next week, with the statement remaining largely unchanged

We look for the Bank to raise its core inflation forecast and lower the unemployment rate projection

• NZ<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate rose to 4.3% in 1Q24, slightly above consensus expectations

Australian retail sales fell 0.4%m/m sa in March, disappointing expectations for a modest increase (J.P. Morgan: +0.3%m/ m, consensus: +0.2%m/m) and reversing last month<sup>’</sup>s gain (Figure 1). Weakness was broad-based, with declines in every category aside from food retailing. The nominal spending data have been choppy through the quarter, and the ABS notes that activity related to the Taylor Swift tour lifted clothing and department store sales in February. This boost was largely unwound in the March data, and NSW and Victorian sales, which were the main beneficiaries of the concerts in February, are now below January<sup>’</sup>s level.

Figure 1: Australia monthly retail sales growth
![](images/05e94865293382de428a745059869318e61eb6c7ae61acf9963714fad23db885.jpg)

In quarterly terms, nominal sales increased just 0.2%q/q in the March quarter, a deceleration from the 4Q result (0.6%q/ q) and sufficient to see the annual rate slow further, to 1.2%oya. With that in mind, the most recent CPI print suggests a meaningful downshift in the retail deflator in early 2024, a positive development for real consumption.

The 1Q inflation data reported outright deflation in the cloth ing/accessories (-1.1%q/q) and furniture (-4.5%q/q) subgroups, only partly offset by stronger outcomes in the food (+0.9%q/q) and cafes/restaurant (+0.5%q/q) indices. In net terms, we forecast a slightly negative retail deflator, which, alongside the nominal spending print, suggests that retail volumes increased 0.3%q/q last quarter.

## RBA maintaining maximum flexibility

The RBA is expected to remain on hold next week, but there is more interest in the message, given the shift in outlook for U.S. rates and the extent to which the local data have rebounded lately. In our view, the language in the prepared statement is well set, in being sufficiently vague that it could suit a number of potential paths for the economy. The press conference and subsequent minutes later in the month, however, should reflect a more hawkish stance than appeared in March.

Governor Bullock will likely be asked in the press conference about hikes, and we expect “not ruling anything in or out<sup>”</sup> to remain a go-to response, as it was for rate cuts. But in the spirit of wanting households to be forewarned for whatever the future brings, we expect the “finely balanced<sup>”</sup> discussion from recent months to be skewed hawkish now, with more open consideration of potential hikes. Similarly, the March minutes had taken out explicit reference to the case for hiking vs. holding, but it would seem appropriate for that debate to return more formally in May.

Since last year<sup>’</sup>s external review of the RBA, the concluding section of policy statements has seemed intended to widen the range of possibilities, rather than refine it. Presumably, this is to foster policy flexibility after the clumsy exit from YCC and calendar guidance created the perception of broken promises.

A clear benefit of this vague guidance is that its meaning has always been in the eye of the beholder. Last meeting, “not ruling anything in or out<sup>”</sup> was viewed dovishly after softer inflation and unemployment readings, and most of the press conference was dedicated to questions about potential rate cuts. December 2024 RBA OIS has since repriced more than 50bp higher in yield, but, for its part, the RBA has consistently warned of the risk of more persistent services inflation, and when asked last meeting whether she was worried about the economy (specifically growth), Bullock stated bluntly, “I<sup>’</sup>m worried about inflation.

The repricing is therefore a case of the market coming toward the RBA<sup>’</sup>s balanced message and forecasts, rather than vice versa, and so the concluding section of next week<sup>’</sup>s statement doesn<sup>’</sup>t have to change much at all. Similarly, the board had already expected it to be “some time yet<sup>”</sup> before inflation was sustainably in the target band, and (March minutes) “before they could have sufficient confidence<sup>”</sup> in achieving that goal.

Week of May 6 - 10

Labor force survey

## RBA to upgrade economic projections

In the quarterly SoMP projections, we look for the staff<sup>’</sup>s core inflation forecasts to be lifted 25bp at year-end (headline less so) and near-term unemployment to be lower by a couple of tenths. The goals look a bit more distant now, but the overall story of balancing priorities and a gradual return to the target in 2025 will remain.

## NZ labor market continues to soften

The NZ unemployment rate rose a little more than expected in 1Q, by 0.3%-pt, to 4.3% (J.P. Morgan and consensus: 4.2%). Employment was softer than expected, with a contraction of 0.2%q/q, in part due to weaker participation (a fourtenths drop, to 71.5%). The broader details on underutilization (unemployed + underemployed) were also weaker, with a 0.5%-pt increase, to 11.2% (Figure 2). Excluding the immediate pandemic period, this is the highest rate since 2019. Since the trough, unemployment has risen 1.1%-pt.

Figure 2: NZ Underemployment and underutilisation
![](images/dbef308b38c9f68e156fb4710e500faea943e1bbdb9d764c2469eb9de2dad9c9.jpg)

Business survey indicators of labor demand and labor availability suggest that the upward trend in unemployment will continue, while the pace of core inflation and arithmetic for the coming quarters make it very likely that the inflation target band is achieved in 2H this year. Combined with mounting financial stability concerns (see this week<sup>’</sup>s RBNZ FSR), we continue to see the RBNZ pivoting to cuts soon, and forecast a first easing in August.

Consistent with the underutilization dynamic, hours worked were relatively soft, up 0.4%q/q in the household survey. The annual rate is 2%oya, which might sound perky, but falls well short of the supply side from working-age population growth, which is running at 3.1%oya. The LCI measure of private sector wages ex-overtime rose 0.8%q/q. This is quite a bit slower than 4Q, and while the trend has been choppy, through the quarterly noise, the trend slowdown is clear; annual wage growth peaked at 4.5%oya in early <sup>‘</sup>23 and has slowed now to 3.8%. Similar business survey relationships to that described for unemployment above suggest that wage growth is headed to around 3% over the next year.
