## Greater China

• China: Politburo meeting focused on policy continuity

• April PMIs suggest solid manufacturing activity while service sectors moderate

Industrial profits fell 3.5%oya in March on moderated IP growth and intensified PPI deflation

Hong Kong SAR: 1Q24 GDP beat expectations; revising up full-year GDP growth forecast to 3.2%y/y; retail sales disappointed in March

• Taiwan: 1Q24 GDP in line with expectations; further solid gain in April manufacturing PMI

The CPC (Communist Party of China) Politburo held a conference on 30 April to assess economic conditions and lay out policy guidance. The Politburo also announced that the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum of the 20<sup>th</sup> CPC Party Congress will be held in July, with the theme of comprehensively deepening the reforms and promoting China<sup>’</sup>s modernization.

In assessing the current economic conditions, the Politburo meeting noted the good start in 1Q economic performance, stating that the positive momentum has been accumulating, social expectations have improved and high-quality development has progressed steadily. The Politburo also pointed out key challenges faced by the economy, e.g., insufficient domestic demand, operating pressure in the corporate sector, potential risks in key areas, and rising complexity and uncertainty in the external environment. Overall, it maintains a balanced view, but stronger-than-expected real GDP growth in 1Q is encouraging. In terms of policy guidance, the Politburo emphasized policy continuity and the need to avoid a loss of momentum in the coming quarters. New development and high-quality growth remain the main theme of policy guidance.

On fiscal policy, there is no change in the magnitude of fiscal policy support (vs. what has been laid out in the NPC meeting), yet the Politburo called for early issuance of ultra-long central government bonds (1 trillion yuan this year, most likely will start to issue in the current quarter), accelerated issuance of special government bonds (3.9 trillion yuan special local government bonds this year) and the maintenance of sufficient intensity in fiscal expenditure. This is mainly to avoid a repeat of the fiscal drag last year that contributed to a rapid loss of growth momentum from 1Q to 2Q.

On monetary policy, the Politburo called for flexible use of interest rate and RRR policy instruments to lower the funding cost to the real economy. This supports our view of further rate cuts and RRR cuts. However, the room for further rate cuts and RRR cuts is modest, and the timing and pace of

interest rate cuts may depend on the NIM pressure by banks and the repricing of the Fed rate outlook (and the impact on the CNY).

On industry policy, productivity increase (“new quality productive forces<sup>”</sup>) is the key to high-quality growth, and the priority remains emerging and advanced manufacturing. The government is excited about the success in several new economy sectors (new energy, battery and NEV), calls for steady progress in green/low-carbon development, hoping to replicate the success in other new economy sectors. However, our concern is the unequal treatment between manufacturing and service upgrade, which does not seem to be addressed at this meeting.

On boosting domestic demand, the Politburo meeting called for implementation of the action plan for equipment replacement and upgrade, as well as the trade-in program for targeted consumer goods. However, recent policy announcements do not clarify the magnitude and funding sources for these programs, and overall the policy bias to support investment (vs. consumption) seems to have remained in place. To boost domestic demand, the Politburo also called for income growth for low-middle-income families and improvement in the social safety net.

Housing is raised as a major source of risk. The Politburo meeting reiterated the importance of ensuring home completion and the continuation of city-specific housing policy adjustments, meanwhile emphasizing a new development model for the housing market and policy measures to absorb inventory stocks and improve new home supply. In our interpretation, further housing policy relaxation will take place, but not sufficient to lead to a bottoming out of housing activity in the near term. The new development model of the housing market lies in public housing and rental housing. Recent experience with trade-in programs (e.g., acquisition of unfinished projects or existing secondary homes and conversion into public housing or rental housing) is interesting, and a key question is whether any institutionalized funding support can be introduced to support public housing.

On regional developments, the Politburo meeting emphasized the integration of the Yangtze River Delta (including Shanghai, Jiangsu, Zhejiang and Anhui) as a pioneer in promoting industry upgrades, higher-level openness and coordinated growth and development.

## April PMIs suggest solid mfg activity

China<sup>’</sup>s April manufacturing PMIs held up better than expected, suggesting solid manufacturing activity. Meanwhile, the NBS service PMI fell significantly, suggesting that the 1Q recovery in service activity and domestic demand may lose

momentum going into 2Q. The NBS manufacturing PMI fell by a modest 0.4-pt, to 50.4 in April (Figure 1). Production activity seems solid, as the output component rose notably by 0.7-pt, to 52.9. Caixin manufacturing PMI beat expectations and rose 0.3-pt, to 51.4, with the output component up 0.3-pt, to 53.1. The export sector seems to support solid industrial activity. The fifth consecutive monthly increase in the export orders component of the export-oriented Caixin manufacturing PMI hints at constructive near-term outlook on export sector activity.

Figure 1: China manufacturing PMIs
![](images/a8348f4687f6b6497318d486ae9b54d22b3bf92bd93aa31a1c183e650ca0516f.jpg)
Source: NBS, S&P Global

The NBS non-manufacturing PMI moderated in April, falling 1.8-pt, to 51.2. The service activity component fell notably by 2.1-pt, to 50.3, partly reflecting the drag from sluggish real estate services. The impact of macro policy support appears to continue to come through, as the NBS construction activity index edged up 0.1-pt, to 56.3 in April, adding to the jump of 2.7-pt in March.

Following the strong 1Q GDP report, our baseline forecasts look for some moderation in 2Q growth (4.0% q/q saar). Signals from the April PMIs reinforce our outlook for moderating 2Q growth, along with the ongoing trend of divergent sector growth patterns (solid industrial production vs. moderating service activity; solid export sector outlook vs. softer domestic sentiments and lingering housing market weakness).

## Industrial profits fell 3.5%oya in March

The NBS announced that total industrial profits (including all industrial companies with annual sales from principal business exceeding 20 million yuan) grew 4.3%oya in January-March (Figure 2), down from 10.2%oya growth in January-February. The mid-single-digit growth rate came off a low base, as industrial profits fell 21.4%oya in January-March 2023. For the month of March, the NBS estimated that industrial profits fell 3.5%oya. Meanwhile, sales revenue grew 2.3%oya in January-March 2024, and cost of sales increased by 2.5%oya. Profit margin rose to 4.86%, compared with 4.70% in January-February. By ownership type, profits of

HMT and foreign enterprises and private enterprises outperformed, growing 18.1%oya and 5.8%oya, respectively.

March industrial profits growth was dragged on by the belowexpectation IP growth (+4.5%oya, or -0.8%m/m sa by our estimate) and intensified PPI deflation (-2.8%oya). According to the NBS, the equipment manufacturing sector<sup>’</sup>s profits grew 18.0%oya in January-March, contributing 4.9%pt to overall industrial profit growth. Leading sectors included electronic equipment, transportation equipment and automobile sectors. Meanwhile, the high-tech manufacturing sector<sup>’</sup>s profits grew 29.1%oya in January-March, with telecommunication terminal equipment, integrated circuits, display equipment, and computer manufacturing the leading sectors.

Figure 2: China industrial enterprises' profits and sales revenue
![](images/0244b7d4110bc6a413344d9ad126e70b0949abb6693594bbc8f07786e01020a2.jpg)

## Hong Kong: 1Q24 GDP beat expectations

Hong Kong SAR<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP (advanced estimate) significantly beat expectations, rising 2.7%oya in real terms (JPM: 0.6%oya; consensus: 0.8%oya). Seasonally adjusted, 1Q GDP rose 9.5%q/q saar after an 0.8%q/q saar rise in 4Q23 (Figure 3). Real private consumption expenditure (PCE) increased 1.0%oya following the 3.5%oya growth in 4Q23. Government consumption expenditure fell 3.0%oya in 1Q24, narrowing from the 5.2%oya decline in 4Q23. Goods exports and imports continued to grow, rising 6.7%oya and 3.2%oya, respectively. Exports and imports of services grew further, increasing 8.1%oya and 17.6%oya, respectively. Gross domestic fixed capital formation (GFCF) rose by a modest 0.3%oya, compared with +17.5%oya in 4Q23. HK C&SD revised up full-year 2023 real GDP growth to 3.3%y/y (previously: 3.2%y/y). For 1Q24, we estimate that the 2.7%oya growth was contributed to mainly by net exports (+3.2%pt), while consumption contributed +0.2%pt and investment dragged by 0.7%pt.

Following the upside surprise in 1Q24 GDP data, we revise up the 2024 full-year growth forecast to 3.2%y/y (previously 2.9%y/y). We expect full-year growth to be supported by consumption and net exports, while investment remains a drag.

Figure 3: Hong Kong real GDP growth
![](images/0fbbfc30806cd1edb0aed0eceff717d6567c6d78725ad95de383f9f73aab8c1e.jpg)

## Retail sales disappointed in March

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s retail sales volume came in below expectations in March, falling 8.6%oya (Figure 4). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that retail sales volume fell 3.1%m/m sa, paying back the 3.6%m/m sa gain in February. In value terms, retail sales fell 7.0%oya in March on a 2.9%m/m sa loss. We pointed out last month that March retail sales might see some payback following the Lunar New Year, but the magnitude was larger than expected. For 1Q24 as a whole, retail sales volume fell 3.0%oya (or -6.5%q/q saar), while retail sales value fell 1.3%oya (or -7.2%q/q saar). The overall softening trend is consistent with the PCE data in the 1Q24 GDP report, as 1Q24 PCE growth narrowed to 1.0%oya, compared with a 3.5%oya rise in 4Q23.

Figure 4: Hong Kong retail sales and private consumption
![](images/6bb285720f9496074fa37140d6cfd946ccf6e1bf0be660893e0b3843b3f35f0a.jpg)
Source: HKC&SD, J.P. Morgan

Retail sales volume of most retail categories contracted in both m/m sa terms and year-over-year terms in March, reflecting payback following the Lunar New Year and the imbalance between inbound tourism and outbound HK residents. Looking ahead, we believe inbound tourism will still be the driving factor of consumption growth in HK, yet the imbalance between inbound and outbound visitor recovery may continue to weigh on HK<sup>’</sup>s consumption. Meanwhile, the low-unemployment environment, household income growth, together with various promotional campaigns and activities launched by the government, should continue to benefit the retail sector. In the near term, we expect retails sales to recover modestly.

## Taiwan: 1Q24 GDP in line with expectations

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s advanced 1Q24 GDP was in line with expectations, rising 6.51%oya (implying modest sequential growth of 1.1% q/q saar following a strong growth pace in the previous three quarters, Figure 5). Beneath the modest sequential growth in headline GDP, exports of goods and services picked up in 1Q24 (16.5% q/q saar), along with a steady domestic consumer spending trend (2.9% q/q saar). Significant expansion in the imports of goods and services (24.0% q/q saar) came as a drag on headline GDP. Following the contraction in recent quarters, domestic capex shows a moderate recovery in 1Q24. The tech sector<sup>’</sup>s inventory cycle seems to be normalizing lately following significant de-stocking since early 2023.

Figure 5: Taiwan real GDP growth
![](images/fe4b3c85e5293006d1aa7f978261dac2f1b6a7ee929361b9a83724393e22a16f.jpg)

We look for a steady upward trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech exports in the coming quarters, while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s de-stocking pressure eases. The constructive outlook on global goods demand, supported by resilient corporate sector fundamentals and solid labor income trends, bodes well for Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export sector activity. Tentative signs of recovery in the domestic capex trend are encouraging, reflecting Taiwan manufacturers<sup>’</sup> improving sentiment on the export sector outlook. On the domestic front, recent easing in employment growth, and hence household income, may somewhat restrain the pace of domestic demand growth going ahead.

We expect the Taiwan economy to show steady sequential growth, with 2.3% q/q saar growth in 2Q and average 2.6% q/ q saar in 2H. Our forecast for full-year 2024 growth remains unchanged, at 4.3%yoy.

## Further solid gain in April mfg PMI

The latest signals suggest further solid gains in headline manufacturing PMI in April, which rose 0.9-pt, to 50.2 (Figure 6), adding to the uptick of 0.7-pt in March and registering the highest reading since May 2022, reinforcing the industrial sector<sup>’</sup>s constructive cyclical momentum.

Week of May 6 - 10

Figure 6: Global and Taiwan manufacturing PMIs
![](images/e4d00f38eeb38f7a77a3f80d7263628cf9f2d15fd57bb133e83342b9ed2fbce7.jpg)

The latest signals from Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI continue to be encouraging. Regarding the cyclical momentum in the industrial sector, as the headline manufacturing PMI increased a further 0.9-pt, to 50.2 in April, it has returned to the highest level since May 2022. In particular, the gain in the output component (up 2.7-pt, to 51.4 in April) hints at solid near-term momentum in industrial production activity. Regarding external demand conditions, signals from the manufacturing PMI export orders component have been rather benign, with a further gain of 0.5-pt, to 49.3 in April, the highest reading since March 2023, hinting at a constructive near-term outlook on external demand. Meanwhile, regarding the latest trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inventory cycle, the manufacturing PMI finished goods inventory indicator rose 2.2-pt, to 50.1 in April, suggesting that manufacturers may be resuming some inventory building following a significant inventory correction in recent quarters.

The long-form nomenclature for references to China; Hong Kong; and Taiwan within this research material is Mainland China; Hong Kong SAR (China); and Taiwan (China).

## Korea

• IP fell sharply in March, and PMI fell in April, suggesting a slower 2Q

• Customs exports turned up in April broadly across tech and non-tech

• CPI inflation stepped down in April to 2.9%oya

This week<sup>’</sup>s activity data were mixed, with a turn up in April customs exports vs. falls in March IP and the April manufacturing PMI. After strong 1Q GDP growth, we expect overall GDP growth to moderate notably in 2Q as a correction. March IP<sup>’</sup>s sharp downturn may imply the weaker 2Q manufacturing sector performance vs. a strong 1Q. That said, the medium-term growth trend through 2024 should remain positive, notably with a continued tech sector recovery.

## IP to trend down in 2Q

Korea<sup>’</sup>s industrial production fell 3.2%m/m, sa after a 2.9% rise in February. In the three-month trend, IP contracted 1.6%3m3m, saar against the strong 1Q manufacturing GDP growth (5.0%q/q, saar), so the negative signal from IP is tempered by the strong 1Q gain in the less volatile manufacturing GDP. That said, the IP trend suggests a moderation of manufacturing GDP growth into 2Q, confirming our earlier call change for weaker 2Q overall real GDP growth.

Figure 1: Real GDP and non-farm all-industry output
![](images/26bf7e081642eb8d59242dd00f830db429d6bfe63b30ee2162fd0ea9d4b65223.jpg)

Meanwhile, non-manufacturing activities also broadly fell in March, including service (-0.8%m/m, sa) and construction (-8.7%) production. If we aggregate manufacturing and nonmanufacturing production, then the monthly GDP proxy indicator (non-farm all-industry output index) fell 2.1%m/m, sa after rising for four months, and the three-month trend growth stood at 2.6%3m/3m, saar.

Looking ahead, we expect the non-farm all-industry output index<sup>’</sup>s trend growth to decelerate in 2Q following March<sup>’</sup>s weakness, consistent with our 2Q real GDP growth outlook of 0.5%q/q, saar to correct after the strong 1Q print (Figure 1).

## Customs exports rose in April

Customs exports rose 4.2%m/m, sa in April after falling for two months. The three-month sequential growth turned up modestly, to 1.4%3m/3m, saar, to suggest a recovery in nominal exports growth in 2Q after 1Q<sup>’</sup>s correction (Figure 2). We expect the customs exports quarterly growth to resume in mid-year, led by continuing tech product exports growth.

Figure 2: Korea customs exports
![](images/659e18335e78391edeb1ed12863cadc3665b31f54ded4d6159c26ac5dcb04b3f.jpg)

In product details, both tech (+2.7%m/m, sa) and non-tech (+4.9%m/m, sa) exports rose in April after March<sup>’</sup>s fall. Among tech products, semiconductor exports rose 1.8% in April, and display exports rose 7.3%. Non-tech exports growth was notable in machinery (11.6%m/m, sa), automobile (8.0%m/m, sa) and petrochemical (6.9%m/m, sa) products. In the three-month trend, tech exports growth is still far outpacing non-tech exports trend growth, as tech exports rose 20.8%3m/3m, saar, while non-tech exports contracted 5.7%3m/3m, saar.

## PMI fell, but with more benign details

Figure 3: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/753605cd179cf05714df3bca64508d62b1808bf9ab1eead6d670743c067ecc2f.jpg)

The manufacturing PMI fell 0.4pt to an eight-month low of 49.4 in April, after running 50-51 in the past half-year. The PMI decline suggests a likely near-term slowdown in manufacturing as a gradual correction, aligning with the actual outturns (monthly IP turned to a modest sequential contraction in the latest March report after robust growth over the past

year). That said, the PMI details were relatively more encouraging, as the key sub-indices of output and new orders rather inched up in the month. This could be a sign that the coming slowdown in manufacturing should be mild and unlikely to lead to a sharp and persistent drop (Figure 3).

## CPI inflation stepped down in April

CPI inflation fell to 2.9%oya in April after recording a 3.1% print in the previous two months, marking below expectations. Core CPI inflation excluding energy and food prices fell to 2.3%oya from 2.4% in March, to continue the gradually stabilizing trend. If seasonally adjusted, headline CPI rose 0.1%m/m, sa and core CPI rose 0.2% in April, and the sequential growth remained at around the 2% annualized pace in both headline (+2.3%3m/3m, saar) and core (2.1%3m/3m, saar) CPI.

Figure 4: Inflation forecast
![](images/021a682f7754f932b7d437b173df428493f8cf6ebf0164f1de7226761f9cc298.jpg)

Looking ahead, we expect May<sup>’</sup>s monthly CPI gain to turn modestly stronger than the April CPI gain due to stronger oil product price gains. The over-year-ago inflation should hover around an upper-2% pace by July (Figure 4). Updating for the April CPI data, our tracking of 2024<sup>’</sup>s annual CPI inflation remains at 2.6%y/y, in line with the Bank of Korea<sup>’</sup>s February forecast.

## ASEAN

• Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s trade balance continues narrowing in March

Narrowing reflects solid capital goods imports and ongoing deterioration in energy balance

• March CPI comes in below expectations, limited evidence of broadening

Expect Malaysia’s policy rate to remain on hold at 3%, other tools to mitigate external monetary conditions

Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s March data was similar to the recent past, with domestic capex remaining resilient. However, the capex spillover into labor markets has been modest. Together with the ongoing supply-side adjustments from subsidy rationalization this suggests limited pricing power, evident in the limited impact on core inflation in March. In our view, this environment leaves the central bank on pause, and our forecast is that the Overnight Policy Rate (OPR) will remain at 3% through 2024 despite the high for long external rates environment.

Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s response to the tightening in external conditions has been multi-faceted, including FX intervention and other administrative measures. With net FX reserves now close to its reserve adequacy levels, a further tightening in administrative measures cannot be ruled out. Such an arrangement reduces the need to lean on domestic rates to counter changes in interest rate differentials and is evident in the stability of the interbank rates despite the rise in DM yields.

## March trade balance continues narrowing

Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s trade surplus expanded from US\$2.4 billion in February to US\$2.7 billion in March, coming in higher than expectations. In seasonally adjusted terms, the trade surplus came in at US\$2.0 billion (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Malaysia trade balance
![](images/35610aa623db4cfd3ebdb9a04f558932ade6c6be489de1bb06a17faf5c9558c2.jpg)

There is an ongoing narrowing in the trade surplus. While the recent narrowing has been led by a rise in imports of capital goods, there has also been a more structural decline in the commodity balance, categorized under SITC 0-4, which has been masked by rise in the manufactured goods surplus, under SITC 7-9, which now looks to be reversing (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Malaysia trade balance by composition US\$ billion, 3mma, sa, ar
![](images/b791b11aff68e4c3ccffa1bd36377d94e2cbd5545eb2f64db6052a2c550e4359.jpg)

Within the commodity balances, which comprises crude palm oil (CPO, subsumed under vegetable oils), mineral fuels and other crude material, it is primarily the decline in the mineral fuels balance that has led the narrowing in the commodity balance (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Malaysia commodity trade balance US\$ billion, 3mma, sa, ar
![](images/1b29d76c86a0685bbdf8cdb6f9befdb3f1804e406bf8993f11ada3baf4fd3fbf.jpg)

## Commodity balance deteriorates

Within mineral fuels, it is specifically the rise in net imports of coal and crude oil that has led the narrowing (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Malaysia net exports of mineral fuels US\$ billion, 3mma, ar
![](images/be7bbf3984c14164d8b2218e828fe7db12026bf4cb816a22db00f65a317e1b1e.jpg)

Figure 8: Malaysia core CPI and OPR %oya and %p.a., eop

Figure 5: Malaysia crude oil trade mn MT, 6mma, ar, both scales

In particular, Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s crude oil export volumes have slowed since 2020 while imports have moved up strongly (Figure 5). It is not clear what has precipitated the rise in imports but the conclusion remains the same; that Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s energy balance has been on a declining trend since its peak in 2011 and could be entering into a persistent deficit.

![](images/0635eaaf70d2b61375648ba3db61045e64172d8cead631d3875e6a2a7e19458b.jpg)

Although natural gas export volumes have risen this year, this has not been sufficient to offset the deterioration in the crude oil balance (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Malaysia natural gas exports
![](images/b2aeec9099fdf8e54e42d9677a1032a4d1949740279b6561eb2459b46a3c8594.jpg)

## March core CPI subdued, BNM on hold

Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s March consumer prices rose 1.8%oya (J.P. Morgan 1.8%oya and consensus 2.1%oya). Core CPI, which excludes the most volatile fresh food items and administered prices of goods and services, rose 1.7%oya (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Malaysia CPI
![](images/8ece2ce2759b0c1751845fb1d2e0f36c5bf247014fa6394163d588e61d2397b3.jpg)

Importantly, the March CPI reading affirms our view that the impact of subsidy and price rationalization has not broadened into core CPI. This reflects – in our view – the limited domestic pricing power in Malaysia given the supply-side nature of the price adjustment and is evident in the normalization of core prices in March.

Given BNM<sup>’</sup>s focus on underlying price stability, we continue to expect the central bank to hold the policy rate at 3%, unless there is a material acceleration in core inflation, which is not our baseline (Figure 8).

![](images/7d6c74cfae6e35701fa1529e84fe80bd7b8c8d7567ad9b4e6c97a6597a69c8fa.jpg)

A high-for-long rates environment in the DM places greater focus on the financial accounts, which in Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s case have generally been in deficit. These pressures have been evident in the exchange rate, with the NEER falling by 3.4% since end-2022. Although Bank Negara has been able to manage these pressures, the policy degrees of freedom to continue doing so remain unclear, given the low level of net FX reserves (Figure 9).

Figure 9: Malaysia net FX reserves and NEER
![](images/5c4bd615f77594957670b283d9ad8aea6f7fbf2bcb1ad55c4f3b86249023266b.jpg)

Moreover, BNM has tended to separate external financial conditions from domestic and thus is not expected to respond to the stronger US\$ via rates – reflected in the relative stability in the 3-mo. KLIBOR spread with the OPR - and instead has used other tools in place of interest rates (Figure 10).

%p.a., eop, both scales

Morgan forecasts

## India

• Bank credit growth continues unabated

• Overall personal growth remains strong, but breakdown of unsecured vs. secured shifts...

• ...with unsecured easing, while the latter picks up

• Signs of real estate upswing broadening

Bank credit growth maintained its strength in March. The breakdown reveals a shift in the sector dynamics: Personal loan growth remains one of the primary drivers, but there has been a moderation in unsecured lending growth following the RBI<sup>’</sup>s tightening of macro-prudential measures. Meanwhile, secured lending (particularly housing loans) has experienced an upturn. The pickup in housing loans has been met with strong growth in commercial real estate loans, suggesting that the real estate cycle is broadening.

## Bank credit growth still buoyant

Bank credit continued its unabated growth, printing a strong 16.3% oya in March, slightly lower than 16.5% in February (Figure 1). For the fiscal year 2023-24, this amounts to a firming in credit growth to 15.7% from 14.3% oya in 2022- 23. At the same time, nominal GDP growth has slowed from 14.2% in 2022-23 to 9.1%, indicative of credit deepening. This is best seen in the bank credit to nominal GDP ratio, which, at 1.7, is the highest in 15 years.

Figure 1: Bank Credit growth
![](images/f16a089f250149872feed63b8a75e9231ac8dd3cbdd0d7f526103a6cf93b3da8.jpg)

Figure 2: Unsecured Loans
![](images/61aacad4e9a3531b120cb18260d053e6bb685eee020a3288a2e67f9ca0315811.jpg)

The data on sector bank credit reveals a more nuanced picture. Personal loans remain a significant contributor to credit expansion overall, but there are early signs of slowdown in unsecured lending. Unsecured loans drove the pickup in personal loan growth in 2023-24, but have tapered off in recent months, dropping to 18.5% oya in March from over 20% in February (Figure 2). Sequentially, unsecured lending decelerated from 1.1% in February to 0.8% m/m sa in March. Credit card loans, a subset of unsecured lending, also slowed sharply, from 31% in February to 26% in March after averaging 33% in the three months leading up to February. However, overall growth in personal loans has not slowed meaningfully (Figure 3), as some of the slowdown in unsecured lending has been offset by a pickup in housing loans (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Personal Loans
![](images/2ef2fd4c21fe89272e3a91335ed6d7aca0e1018fbd6b8c72352677977c90b65a.jpg)

Figure 4: Credit card loans vs housing loans
![](images/61cb330d122bff36de2f6e9c24019d3c5deb1dab9ea750fd1636853508f7ddac.jpg)

Figure 5: Commercial Real Estate Loans
![](images/da7bcf2ee2e1e3ce3f556861b64bff96eff4f934e4b0c93416aa26ca99be257e.jpg)

## Commercial RE shows strong momentum

Credit growth in the commercial real estate (CRE) sector has accelerated, with strong sequential gains over the past few months (Figure 5). In %oya terms, there was a slight drop in March, to 23% oya from 25% oya in February, but growth remains much higher than the \~18% oya in the three months leading up to February. The growth in CRE loans has been driven by a surge in office leasing, in turn led by a resurgence of global capability centers (GCCs). The pickup in housing loans, coupled with strong growth in CRE loans, underscores our view that India is in the midst of an upswing in the real estate cycle.

## IP expected to soften in March

Following three consecutive months of strong sequential gains in IP growth, averaging \~2% m/m sa, we expect some payback in March. We anticipate a contraction of 1.6% m/m sa, underscored by a sequential contraction in core IP (-1.2% m/m sa), as well as auto production. In over-year-ago terms, we expect IP to print at \~5% in March, down from February<sup>’</sup>s 5.7%.
